Dynamic Causality in Event Structures Thanks: Supported by the DFG Research Training Group SOAMED
Youssef Arbach, David S. Karcher, Kirstin Peters, Uwe Nestmann

Abstract
. Event Structures (ESs) address the representation of direct relationships between individual events, usually capturing the notions of causality and conflict. Up to now, such relationships have been static, i.e. they cannot change during a system run. Thus, the common ESs only model a static view on systems. We make causality dynamic by allowing causal dependencies between some events to be changed by occurrences of other events. We first model and study the case in which events may entail the removal of causal dependencies, then we consider the addition of causal dependencies, and finally we combine both approaches in the so-called Dynamic Causality ESs . For all three newly defined types of ESs, we study their expressive power in comparison to the well-known Prime ESs, Dual ESs, Extended Bundle ESs, and ESs for Resolvable Conflicts . Interestingly, Dynamic Causality ESs subsume Extended Bundle ESs and Dual ESs but are incomparable with ESs for Resolvable Conflicts.



\lmcsheading

1–
Dec. 30, 2015Feb. 27, 2018

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
F Theory of Computation, F.1.1 Models of Computation

1.
Introduction

Motivation.

Modern process-aware systems emphasize the need for flexibility into their design to adapt to changes in their environment
[
22
]
. One form of flexibility is the ability to change the work-flow during the run-time of the system deviating from the default path, due to changes in
regulations or to exceptions.
Such changes could be ad hoc or captured at the build time of the system

[
18
]
. For instance—as taken from

[
22
]
—
during the treatment process, and for a particular patient, a planned computer tomography must not be performed due to the fact that she has a cardiac pacemaker. Instead, an X-ray activity shall be performed.

In this paper, we provide a formal model that can be used for such scenarios, showing what is the
regular
execution path and what is the
exceptional
one
[
18
]
. In the Conclusions section, we highlight the advantages of our model over other static-causality models w.r.t. such scenarios.

Concurrency Model.

In many formalisms for concurrent systems, as e.g. in Petri nets or different process calculi, causality is a derived concept (
[
3
,
10
]
). As a consequence, adding dynamicity to the causality relation usually requires the duplication of modeled actions, i.e., transitions in Petri nets or parts of processes in process calculi. Event Structures (ESs) model causality more directly. They usually address statically defined relationships that constrain the possible occurrences of events, typically represented as
causality
(for precedence) and
conflict
(for choice). An event is a single occurrence of an action; it cannot be repeated. ESs were first used to give semantics to Petri nets
[
23
]
, then to process calculi
[
8
,
16
]
, and recently to model quantum strategies and games
[
26
]
. The semantics of an ES itself is usually provided by the sets of traces compatible with the constraints, or by configuration-based sets of events, possibly in their partially-ordered variant (
posets
).

Overview.

We study the idea—motivated by application scenarios—of events changing the causal dependencies of other events.
In order to deal with dynamicity in causality, usually
duplications of events are used (see e.g.
[
10
]
,
where copies of the same event have the same label, but different
dependencies). In this paper, we want to express dynamic changes of causality more directly without duplications. We allow dependencies to change during a system run, by modifying the causality itself. In this way, we avoid duplications of events, and keep the model simple and more intuitive.
We separate the idea of dropping (shrinking) causality from adding (growing) causality and study each one separately first, and then combine them into so-called Dynamic Causality ESs (DCESs).

Example.

plow
water
plant
harvest
rain
pest infestation
pest control

Figure 1.
An example Dynamic Causality ES (DCES). Events are represented as dots, dependencies as solid arrows. The fact that an event can insert or delete a dependency is represented by an arrow between the event and the dependency, with a filled arrowhead for insertion and an empty arrowhead for deletion. Initially absent dependencies, which may be added, are dotted.

Figure
1
presents an example DCES: In the regular work-flow, after plowing and watering, some crop can be planted and finally harvested. Exceptional behavior changes those dependencies: First, rain deletes the necessity of watering for planting. Second, a pest infestation inserts a new precondition—pest control—for harvesting. Note that pest control could also be done prophylactically, but it becomes mandatory after a pest infestation.

Related Work.

Kuske and Morin in
[
15
]
worked on local independence, using local traces.
There, actions can be independent from each other after a given history.
By contrast, in our work we provide a mechanism for independence of events through the growing and shrinking causality, while this related work abstracts from the way actions become independent.
In
[
20
]
, van Glabbeek and Plotkin introduced Event Structures for Resolvable Conflicts (RCESs), where conflicts can be resolved or created by the occurrence of other events. This dynamicity of conflicts is complementary to our approach.
As visualized in Figure
2
, DCESs and RCESs are incomparable but—similarly to RCESs—DCESs are more expressive than many other types of ESs.
In
[
12
]
, Hildebrandt
et al.
present a generalized version of ESs, DCR-Graphs, for similar purposes as discussed here. They can, e.g., express that a response is needed after an action. Causal dependencies, however, are static in their approach, i.e., the representation of dynamic changes—as present in systems with a regular execution path and some exceptional execution paths—requires the duplication of actions.

Figure 2.
Landscape of Event Structures (newly defined ESs are bold)

In §
2
, we define a relaxed version of
Prime ESs

[
25
]
, denoted as relaxed Prime ESs (rPESs), and recap the definitions for
Bundle ESs
(BESs)
[
16
]
,
Extended Bundle ESs
(EBESs)
[
16
]
,
Dual ES
(DESs)
[
17
]
, and
ESs for Resolvable Conflicts
(RCESs)
[
20
]
.

In §
3
, we investigate the various kinds of semantic models that are used by the aforementioned ESs in order to compare them with respect to their expressive power.

In §
4
, we define
Shrinking causality Event Structures
(SESs); we show that SESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs, have the same expressive power as DESs, and thus are strictly more expressive than rPESs, PESs, BESs,
Flow Event Structures
(FESs)
[
8
]
and
Stable Event Structures
(StESs)
[
25
]
, and are incomparable to EBESs.

In §
5
, we define
Growing causality Event Structures
(GESs); we show that GESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs and strictly more expressive than rPESs and PESs.

In §
6
, we combine both aforementioned concepts within the
Dynamic Causality Event Structures
(DCESs) and show that they are strictly more expressive than EBESs, which are incomparable to SESs and GESs.
Although RCESs are shown to be more expressive than GESs and SESs, they are incomparable with DCESs. To complete the picture, note that the
set-based Higher order Dynamic causality Event Structures
(HDES)
[
13
]
are then strictly more expressive than both RCESs and DCESs (cf. §
6.4
).

We defer some of the more technical lemmata and some technical definitions to the Appendix.
The relations among the various classes of ESs are summarized in Figure
2
, where an arrow from one class to another means that the first is less expressive than the second. Here, bold arrows indicate newly derived results, whereas thin arrows indicate results taken from literature and are augmented with the respective reference. The bold structures are newly defined in this paper and the gray ones are added to complete the picture but are discussed here only briefly.

In §
7
, we summarize the contributions and show the limitations of other static-causality models w.r.t. our example, and conclude with future work.

This paper is an extended version of
[
2
]
, with a special focus on the different type of used equivalences and an alternative, equivalent, and more intuitive transition definition for SESs and GESs. For the DCESs, we omit the old transition definition and only state the new one, which simplifies most proofs. We also omit the conflict relation from the definitions of the GESs and DCESs, as it can there be expressed as a derived concept.

2.
Technical Preliminaries

We investigate the idea of dynamically evolving dependencies between events. Therefore, we want to allow for the occurrence of events to create new causal dependencies between events or to remove such dependencies. We base our extension on (a relaxed variant of) Prime ESs, because it represents a very simple causality model.
In the following, we shortly revisit the main definitions of the types of ESs from the literature that we compare with. ESs are sometimes augmented with labels for events, e.g., to relate several events and an action in another formalism (
[
16
,
8
]
). Since our results are not influenced by the presence of labels, we restrict our attention to unlabeled ESs.

2.1.
Relaxed Prime Event Structures

A Prime Event Structure (PES)
[
25
]
consists of a set of events and two relations describing conflicts and causal dependencies. To cover the intuition that events causally depending on an infinite number of other events can never occur, Winskel
[
25
]
requires PESs to satisfy the
axiom of finite causes
.
Additionally, the enabling relation is assumed to be a partial order, i.e., it is reflexive, transitive, and anti-symmetric. Furthermore, the concept of
conflict heredity
is required: an event
a
that is in conflict with another event
b
is also in conflict with all causal successors of
b
.

If we allow causal dependencies to be added or dropped dynamically, it is hard to maintain the conflict heredity as well as transitivity and reflexivity of enabling.
Thus, we define
relaxed Prime Event Structures
(rPES) where we omit the axiom of conflict heredity (as e.g. in
[
5
]
) and do not require that enabling is a partial order.

{defi}

A
relaxed Prime Event Structure (rPES)
is a triple
\pi=\left(E,\#,\to\right)
, where

•

E
is a set of so-called
events
,

•

\#\subseteq E^{2}
is an irreflexive symmetric relation (the
conflict
relation), and

•

{\to}\subseteq E^{2}
is the
enabling
relation.

Note that, in comparison to PES, we also omit the finite causes property; its intention will instead be provided through the constraint on finite configurations (see, e.g., Definition
4
).
Note that rPESs have the same expressive power as PESs in
[
25
]
w.r.t. finite configurations, since the axiom of finite causes is trivially satisfied for finite configurations and on the other hand the concept of conflict heredity does not influence the expressive power, but only ensures that syntactic and semantic conflicts coincide.

The computation state of a process that is modeled as a rPES is represented by the set of events that have occurred.
Naturally, such sets are required to be consistent with the causality and conflict relations of the given rPES.

{defi}

Let
\pi=\left(E,\#,\to\right)
be a rPES.

A set of events
C\subseteq E
is a
configuration
of
\pi
if it is

•

conflict-free
, i.e.,

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in C\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)
,

•

downward-closed
, i.e.,

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e\!\to\!e^{\prime}\land e^{\prime}\in C\implies e\in C
, and

•

free of enabling cycles
, i.e.,
\to^{\ast}\cap\;C^{2}
is antisymmetric.

We denote the set of configurations of
\pi
by
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
.

For each
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
,
we define the partial order

{\leq_{C}}\mathrel{:=}{{(\to^{*})}\cap{C^{2}}}
.

An event
e
is called
impossible
in a rPES
\pi
if it does not occur in any configuration of
\pi
. Events can be impossible because of (1) enabling cycles, (2) an overlap between the enabling and the conflict relation, or (3) impossible predecessors.

In the relaxed version of the PESs, and in contrast to the original PES, the following two properties do not hold due to impossible events and the lack of conflict heredity.

{defi}

[Fullness and faithfulness,
[
24
,
7
]
]
An ES is called
full
if every event is possible, i.e., occurs in a configuration.
An ES is called
faithful
if the syntactic conflict relation
\#
on possible events coincides with the semantic conflict relation
\#_{s}
given by:

e\mathrel{\#_{s}}e^{\prime}\iff\forall\text{ finite }C\,.\;\{e,e^{\prime}\}\not\subseteq C

Lemma 1
.

There are rPES that are not full or faithful.

Proof 2.1
.

For fullness, consider
\pi=(\{a,b,c\},\emptyset,\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\})
, a rPES with three events, no conflicts, and a dependency cycle. In
\pi
, we only have the empty set as a configuration; thus,
\pi
is not full.

Now for faithfulness, consider
\pi^{\prime}=(\{a,b,c\},\{(a,c)\},\{(a,b)\})
, a rPES with three events, a conflict between
a
and
c
, and where
b
depends on
a
(this is the rPES in Fig.
8
on page
8
). There is no syntactic conflict between
b
and
c
, but there is a semantic conflict because any configuration containing
b
and
c
should both contain
a
, to be downward-closed, and not contain
a
, to be conflict-free; thus,
\pi^{\prime}
is not faithfull.

The set of causes of an event
e
, following the reflexive and transitive closure of the enabling relation backwards, is denoted by
\lceil{e}\rceil
. Moreover, if
D
is a configuration and
d\in D
, then
\lceil{d}\rceil_{D}
is the restriction of
\lceil{d}\rceil
to events in
D
.

{defi}

[
[
9
]
]

Let
\pi=(E,\#,\to)
be a rPES and
e\in E
then

\lceil{e}\rceil:=\left\{e^{\prime}\in E\mid e^{\prime}\to^{\ast}e\right\}.

Let
D\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
and
d\in D
then

\lceil{d}\rceil_{D}:=\left\{d^{\prime}\in D\mid d^{\prime}\leq_{D}d\right\}.

For rPESs, the following lemmas hold.

Lemma 2

(Primality,
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\pi=(E,\#,\to)
be a rPES and
e
be possible in
\pi
. Then:

•

\lceil{e}\rceil_{C}=\lceil{e}\rceil
for all
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
such that
e
in
C
,

•

\lceil{e}\rceil\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
, and

•

\lceil{e}\rceil
is the minimal configuration in
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
containing
e
.

Proof 2.2
.

The property
\lceil{e}\rceil_{C}=\lceil{e}\rceil
follows from the fact that

{\leq_{C}}\mathrel{:=}{{(\to^{*})}\cap{C^{2}}}
,
while the property
\lceil{e}\rceil\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
follows from the fact that there is at least one configuration
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
such that
e\in C
, and that
\lceil{e}\rceil
inherits the required properties from
C
. Minimality is implied by the fact that
\lceil{e}\rceil_{C}=\lceil{e}\rceil
for any
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
, since any configuration
C^{\prime}\subset\lceil{e}\rceil_{C}
would miss some event
e^{\prime}\in\lceil{e}\rceil
and thus would fail to satisfy
\lceil{e}\rceil_{C^{\prime}}=\lceil{e}\rceil
.

Lemma 3

(Stability,
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\pi=(E,\#,\to)
be a rPES and
let
C_{1},C_{2}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
such that
(C_{1}\cup C_{2})\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
.
Then:

•

(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
, and

•

for all
e\in(C_{1}\cap C_{2})
,
we have
\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{1}\cap C_{2}}=\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{1}}=\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{2}}
.

Proof 2.3
.

Assume
C_{1},C_{2},(C_{1}\cup C_{2})\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
. We start by showing that also
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
. The properties that
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})
is conflict-free and
{\to}\cap{(C_{1}\cap C_{2})^{2}}
is acyclic follow from the analogue properties of
(C_{1}\cup C_{2})
, while the downward-closure of
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})
follows from the downward-closure of both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. Therefore
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
. Now, we can use primality to conclude that
\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{1}\cap C_{2}}=\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{1}}=\lceil{e}\rceil_{C_{2}}
for any
e\in(C_{1}\cap C_{2})
.

2.2.
Bundle, Extended Bundle and Dual Event Structures

The rPESs are simple but also limited. They do not allow one to describe an optional or conditional enabling of events. Bundle event structures (BESs)—among others—were designed to overcome these limitations
[
16
]
.

Bundles
are pairs
\left(X,e\right)
, denoted as
X\!\rightarrowtail\!e
, where
X
is a set of events and
e
is the event pointed by that bundle.
A bundle is satisfied when one event of
X
occurs. An event is enabled when all bundles pointing to it are satisfied. This
disjunctive causality
allows for optionality in enabling events.

{defi}

[
[
16
]
]

A
Bundle Event Structure (BES)
is a triple
\beta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
, where

•

E
is a set of
events
,

•

\#\subseteq E^{2}
is an irreflexive symmetric relation (the
conflict
relation), and

•

{\rightarrowtail}\subseteq{\text{P}\!\left(E\right)\times E}
is the

enabling
relation, satisfying the following
stability
condition:

X\!\rightarrowtail\!e
implies that for all

e_{1},e_{2}\in X
with
e_{1}\neq e_{2}
we have

e_{1}\mathrel{\#}e_{2}
.

Figure
3
(a) shows an example of a BES. The solid arrows denote causality, i.e., reflect the enabling relation, the line between the arrows indicates a bundle, and the dashed line denotes a mutual conflict.

a
b
c
(a)
a
b
c
d
e
(b)
a
b
c
(c)

Figure 3.
A Bundle ES, an Extended Bundle ES, and a Dual ES.

A configuration of a BES is again a conflict-free set of events that is
downward-closed. Therefore, the stability condition avoids causal
ambiguity
[
17
]
. To exclude sets of events that result from enabling cycles, we use (event) traces (called
proving sequences
in
[
16
]
).
For a sequence
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
of events, let
\overline{t}=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\right\}
and
t_{i}=e_{1}\cdots e_{i}
for

1\leq{i}\leq{n}
. Let
\epsilon
denote the empty sequence.
Let
\operatorname{B}(e)\;\triangleq\;\left\{X\subseteq E\mid X\!\rightarrowtail\!e\right\}
.

{defi}

[
[
16
]
]

Let
\beta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a BES.

A
trace
is a sequence of distinct events
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
with
\overline{t}\subseteq E
that respects

•

conflicts, i.e.,
\forall 1\leq i,j\leq n\,.\;\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
, and

•

bundle satisfaction,
i.e.,

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\forall X\in\operatorname{B}(e_{i})\,.\;\overline{t_{i-1}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
.

A set of events
C\subseteq E
is a
configuration
of
\beta
if there is a trace
t
such that
C=\overline{t}
.

This trace-based definition of a configuration will be the same for Extended Bundle and Dual ESs.
Let
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\beta\right)
denote the set of traces and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta\right)
the set of configurations of
\beta
.

A partially ordered set, or
poset
, is a pair
\left(A,\leq\right)
, where
A
is a finite set and
\leq
is a
partial order
over
A
.
Posets are used as a semantic model for several kinds of ESs and also other models of concurrency
[
19
]
.
For example, and in contrast to mere configurations, if
A
is a set of
events
, then the poset
\left(A,\leq\right)
does not only record which events have happened, but the order
\leq
also captures their precedence relations.

A poset represents a set of system runs, differing for permutation of independent events. To describe the semantics of the entire ES, families of posets
[
19
]
with a prefix relation are used. According to Rensink
[
19
]
, families of posets provide a convenient underlying structure for models of concurrency, and are at least as expressive as families of configurations.

To obtain the posets of a BES, we augment each of its configurations with a partial order.
Let
\beta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a BES,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta\right)
, and
e,e^{\prime}\in C
. Then
e\prec_{C}e^{\prime}
holds if
\exists X\subseteq E\,.\;e\in X\land X\!\rightarrowtail\!e^{\prime}
.
Let
\leq_{C}
be the reflexive and transitive closure of
\prec_{C}
.
It is proved in
[
16
]
that
\leq_{C}
is a partial order over
C
. Let
\operatorname{P}\!\left(\beta\right)
denote the set of posets of
\beta
.
Each linearization (obeying the defined precedence relations) of a given poset of a BES (or EBES) yields an event
trace
of that structure (
[
16
]
).

The first extension of BESs that we consider are
Extended Bundle Event Structures
(EBESs) from
[
16
]
. The conflict relation
\#
is replaced by a
disabling
relation. An event
e_{1}
disables another event
e_{2}
, meaning that the occurrence of
e_{1}
precludes any subsequent occurrence of
e_{2}
afterwards.
The symmetric conflict
\#
can be modeled through mutual disabling.
Therefore, EBESs are a generalization of BESs, and thus are more expressive
[
16
]
.

{defi}

[
[
16
]
]

An
Extended Bundle Event Structure (EBES)
is a triple
\xi=\left(E,\leadsto,\rightarrowtail\right)
, where

•

E
is a set of
events
,

•

{\leadsto}\subseteq E^{2}
is
the irreflexive
disabling
relation, and

•

{\rightarrowtail}\subseteq\text{P}\!\left(E\right)\times E
is the

enabling
relation satisfying the following
stability
condition:

X\!\rightarrowtail\!e
implies that for all

e_{1},e_{2}\in X
with
e_{1}\neq e_{2}
we have

e_{1}\!\leadsto\!e_{2}
.

Stability again ensures that two distinct events within a bundle set are in mutual disabling.
Figure
3
(b) shows an EBES with the two bundles
\left\{a,c\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!d
and
\left\{b,c\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!d
.
The dashed lines denote again mutual disabling as required by stability. A disabling
d\!\leadsto\!e
, to be read “
e
disables
d
”, is represented by a dashed arrow.

{defi}

[
[
16
]
]

Let
\xi=\left(E,\leadsto,\rightarrowtail\right)
be an EBES.

A
trace
is a sequence of distinct events
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
with
\overline{t}\subseteq E
that respects

•

disabling, i.e.,

\forall 1\leq i,j\leq n\,.\;e_{i}\!\leadsto\!e_{j}\implies i<j
, and

•

bundle satisfaction,
i.e.,

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\forall X\in\operatorname{B}(e_{i})\,.\;\overline{t_{i-1}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
.

We adapt the definitions of configurations and traces of BESs accordingly.
For
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\xi\right)
and
e,e^{\prime}\in C
, let
e\prec_{C}e^{\prime}

if
e\!\leadsto\!e^{\prime}
or

\exists X\in\operatorname{B}(e^{\prime})\,.\;e\in X
.
Again
\leq_{C}
denotes the reflexive and transitive closure of
\prec_{C}
, and
\operatorname{P}\!\left(\xi\right)
denotes the set of posets of
\xi
.

Dual Event Structures
(DESs) are obtained
by dropping the stability condition of BESs. This leads to causal ambiguity, i.e.,
given a trace and one of its events, it is not always possible to determine
which other events caused this event.
The definition of DESs differs between
[
14
]
(based on EBESs) and
[
17
]
(based on BESs).
Here, we rely on
[
17
]
.

{defi}

[
[
17
]
]

A
Dual Event Structure (DES)
is a triple
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
, where

•

E
is a set of
events
,

•

\#\subseteq E^{2}
is an irreflexive symmetric relation (the
conflict
relation), and

•

{\rightarrowtail}\subseteq\text{P}\!\left(E\right)\times E
is the
enabling
relation.

Figure
3
(c) shows a DES with one bundle, namely
\left\{a,b\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!c
. It is a relaxed version of the BES in Figure
3
(a) since the stability condition (which enforced a conflict between
a
and
b
) is dropped, and there is no conflict.

The definitions of traces and configurations are relaxed accordingly.

{defi}
[
[
17
]
]

Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES.

A
trace
is a sequence of distinct events
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}

with
\overline{t}\subseteq E
that respects

•

conflicts, i.e.,

\forall 1\leq i,j\leq n\,.\;\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
, and

•

bundle satisfaction,
i.e.,

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\forall X\in\operatorname{B}(e_{i})\,.\;\overline{t_{i-1}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
.

Because of the causal ambiguity, the definition of
\leq_{C}
is difficult and the behavior of a DES w.r.t. a configuration cannot be described by a single poset anymore.
In
[
17
]
, the authors illustrate various possible interpretations of causality. The authors defined five different intensional posets: liberal, bundle satisfaction, minimal, early and late posets.
They show the equivalence of the behavioral semantics, and that the early causality and trace equivalence coincide. Thus, we concentrate on early causality in the following. The remaining intensional partial order semantics are discussed in Appendix
B
.

To capture causal ambiguity, we have to consider all traces of a configuration to obtain its posets.
In essence, the approach defines causes of events
e
in a given trace
t
as not necessarily uniquely defined sets
U
of events.
Posets are then derived by
c\prec e
for
c\in{U}
for all events in the trace and their possible causes as reflexive-transitive closure of
\prec
.

{defi}

[
[
17
]
]

Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES,
let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
be one of its traces.

For two sets
U_{1},U_{2}
of events of
t
, we refer to
U_{1}
as
earlier
than
U_{2}
, if the largest index in
U_{1}\setminus U_{2}
is smaller than the largest index in
U_{2}\setminus U_{1}
.

A set
U\subseteq{E}
is a
cause
of
e_{i}
in
t
(i.e., for
1\leq i\leq n
), if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists\,1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
,

•

\forall X\in\operatorname{B}(e_{i})\,.\;X\cap U\neq\emptyset
, and

•

U
is the earliest set satisfying the previous two conditions.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{d}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for
t
.

Note that,
for BESs, EBESs, and DESs, families of posets are the most discriminating semantics studied in the literature. So, in these cases, we consider two ESs as behaviorally equivalent if they have the same set of posets.

{defi}

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be either of BESs, EBESs, or DESs. They are called
poset equivalent
, written
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\mu_{2}
if
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
have the same set of posets.

2.3.
Event Structures for Resolvable Conflicts

Event Structures for Resolvable Conflicts (RCES) were introduced in
[
20
]
to generalize former types of ESs and to give semantics to general Petri Nets. They allow us to model the case where
a
and
c
cannot occur together until
b
takes place, i.e., initially
a
and
c
are in conflict, and they stay in conflict until the occurrence of
b
resolves it.
An RCES consists of a set of events and an
enabling
relation between sets of events. The latter serves to provide witnesses for transitions between configurations
X
and
Y
: for each subset
Z
of configuration
Y
, there must be a witnessing subset
W
of the preceding configuration
X
, where
W
enables
Z
. The enabling relation also allows us to implicitly model conflicts between events, as we will see in the example below.

{defi}

[
[
20
]
]

An
Event Structure for Resolvable Conflicts (RCES)
is a pair
\rho=\left(E,\vdash\right)
, where
E
is a set of
events
and
{{\vdash}}\subseteq\text{P}\!\left(E\right)^{2}
is the
enabling relation
.

In
[
20
]
, several versions of configurations are defined. Here, we consider only configurations that are both reachable and finite.

{defi}

[
[
20
]
]

Let
\rho=\left(E,{\vdash}\right)
be an RCES and
X,Y\subseteq E
.
Then:

\displaystyle X\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho}}Y\iff(X\subset Y\land\forall Z\subseteq Y\,.\;\exists W\subseteq X\,.\;W\vdash Z)

If no confusion is possible, we sometimes omit the subscript
\rho
.

The set of
(reachable) configurations
of
\rho
is defined as

\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho\right)=\left\{X\subseteq E\mid\emptyset\mathrel{\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho}}^{*}}X\land X\text{ is finite}\right\}

where
\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho}}^{*}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho}}
.

Note the difference between
\subset
and
\subseteq
in the above definition.

As an example, consider the RCES
\rho=\left(E,{\vdash}\right)
, where
E=\left\{a,b,c\right\}
, with
\left\{b\right\}\vdash\left\{a,c\right\}
, and with
\emptyset\vdash X
iff
X\subseteq E
and
X\neq\left\{a,c\right\}
.
It models the initial conflict between
a
and
c
that can be resolved by
b
.

\emptyset
(\rho)
\left\{a\right\}
\left\{b\right\}
\left\{c\right\}
\left\{a,b\right\}
\left\{b,c\right\}
\left\{a,b,c\right\}
\emptyset
(\rho_{\gamma})
\left\{b\right\}
\left\{a\right\}
\left\{c\right\}
\left\{a,b\right\}
\left\{a,c\right\}
\left\{b,c\right\}
\left\{a,b,c\right\}

Figure 4.
Transition graphs of RCESs with resolvable conflict (
\rho
) and disabling (
\rho_{\gamma}
).

In Figure
4
(
\rho
), the respective transition graph is shown, i.e., the nodes are all reachable configurations of
\rho
and the directed edges represent
\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho}}
. Note that, because of
\{a,c\}\subset\{a,b,c\}
and
\emptyset\not\vdash\left\{a,c\right\}
, there is no transition from
\emptyset
to
\{a,b,c\}
.

We consider two RCESs as equivalent if they have the same transition graphs. Note that, since we consider only
reachable
configurations, the transition equivalence defined below is denoted as
reachable
transition equivalence in
[
20
]
.

{defi}

[
[
20
]
]
Two RCESs

\rho_{1}=\left(E_{1},{\vdash}_{1}\right)
and

\rho_{2}=\left(E_{2},{\vdash}_{2}\right)

are called
(reachable) transition equivalent
,
written
\rho_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho_{2}
,
if
E_{1}=E_{2}

and
{\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho_{1}}}\cap\left(\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{1}\right)\right)^{2}}={\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho_{2}}}\cap\left(\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{2}\right)\right)^{2}}
.

For RCESs, transition equivalence is the most discriminating sensible semantics studied in the literature, so we consider two RCESs
behaviorally equivalent
if they have the same reachable transition graphs.
In analogy with poset equivalence, we may lift this notion to compare arbitrary types of ESs that are equipped with a transition relation.

{defi}

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be of either type of ESs with transition relation. They are called
transition equivalent
, written
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu_{2}
, if they have the same reachable transition graphs.

3.
Semantics of Event Structures

We observe that, due to the different expressive power of the aforementioned types of event structures, different kinds of semantic models are used to compare elements of the same type. Relaxed Prime ESs are compared w.r.t. finite configurations. BESs, EBESs, and DESs are compared w.r.t. families of posets. RCESs are compared w.r.t. their transition graphs. In order to build a hierarchy later on, we relate these semantical models. Fortunately, the three semantical models only grow with the expressive power of the respective ESs. In other words, these three semantical models form a hierarchy on their own with respect to their power to discriminate event structures.

3.1.
Posets

Now, we naturally extend the definition of posets to rPESs. Let
\pi=\left(E,\#,\to\right)
be a rPES,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi\right)
, and
e,e^{\prime}\in C
. Then
e\prec_{C}e^{\prime}
if
e\!\to\!e^{\prime}
. Again
\leq_{C}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\prec_{C}
. Then let
\operatorname{P}\!\left(\pi\right)
denote the sets of posets of
\pi
. By Definition
2.2
,
\pi_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\pi_{2}
tells us that
\operatorname{P}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{P}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)
. Note that for rPESs, there is no need to distinguish between different definitions of posets as done in
[
17
]
for DESs. Accordingly, posets in rPESs are a direct consequence of the enabling relation. Since the effect of the enabling relation is already captured by the set of configurations, the consideration of posets does not add discriminating power in this case.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\pi_{1}=\left(E_{1},\#_{1},\to_{1}\right)
and
\pi_{2}=\left(E_{2},\#_{2},\to_{2}\right)
be rPESs.

Then
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)\Longleftrightarrow\pi_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\pi_{2}
.

Proof 3.1

(Proof
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\leq_{C}^{i}
be the reflexive and transitive closure of
\prec_{C}
w.r.t.
\pi_{i}
. Thus,
\leq_{C}^{i}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\to_{i}
restricted on events in
C
.

The implication
\pi_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\pi_{2}\Longrightarrow\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)
is straightforward, since, by Definition,
P\in\operatorname{P}\!\left(\pi_{i}\right)
if and only if
P=\left(C,\leq_{C}^{i}\right)
for some
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{i}\right)
.

We prove the reverse implication
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)\Longrightarrow\pi_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\pi_{2}
. Assume
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)
. Suppose that in some
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{i}\right)
, the two partial order relations
\leq_{C}^{i}
are different. Without loss of generality, we may assume that this is because for some
e,e^{\prime}\in C
, we have
e\leq_{C}^{1}e^{\prime}
and
e\not\leq_{C}^{2}e^{\prime}
.
Let
\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C}^{i}=\left\{e^{\prime\prime}\in C\mid e^{\prime\prime}\leq_{C}^{i}e^{\prime}\right\}
(see Definition
2.1
). Then
e\in\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C}^{1}
and
e\notin\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C}^{2}
.
Let now
\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil^{1}=\left\{e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}\mid e^{\prime\prime}\to_{1}^{*}e^{\prime}\right\}
. By Lemma
2
(primality),
\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C}^{1}=\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil^{1}
and
\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil^{1}=\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C^{\prime}}^{1}
for any configuration
C^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)
that contains
e^{\prime}
. This implies
\lceil{e^{\prime}}\rceil_{C}^{2}\notin\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)
, which contradicts the hypothesis
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{1}\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\pi_{2}\right)
.

Since the definitions of posets coincide with respect to early, liberal, bundle satisfaction, minimal, and late causality, this result holds with respect to posets defined by either of these notions.
Also note that, as shown in
[
17
]
, posets are more discriminating for DESs with respect to liberal, bundle satisfaction, minimal, and late causality; but coincide with sets of configurations for BESs and DESs, where posets are defined based on early causality.

3.2.
Transition Graphs

For a transition-based ES with a few additional properties, there is a natural embedding into RCESs.

{defi}

Let
\mu
be an ES with event set
E
and transition relation
\mapsto
that is

•

strict
, i.e.,
X\!\mapsto\!Y
implies
X\subset Y
, and

•

dense
, i.e.,
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\subset Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
implies that
X^{\prime}\!\mapsto\!Y^{\prime}\implies X\!\mapsto\!Y

for all configurations
X,Y,X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
of
\mu
.

Then,
\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right):=\left(E,\left\{({X},{Z})\mid\exists Y\subseteq E\,.\;X\!\mapsto\!Y\land Z\subseteq Y\right\}\right)
.

By Definition
2.3
, the resulting structure
\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right)
is a RCES.

We show that
\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right)
is transition equivalent to
\mu
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mu
be an ES that is strict and dense according to Definition
3.2
.

Then,

{\mathrel{\mapsto_{\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right)}}}={\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mu}}}
.

Proof 3.2
.

Assume
X\!\mapsto_{\mu}\!Y
. Then, by Definition
3.2
,
X\subset Y
and
X\vdash Z
for all
Z\subseteq Y
. Then, by Definition
2.3
, also
X\mathrel{\mapsto_{\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right)}}Y
.

Assume
X\mathrel{\mapsto_{\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\mu\right)}}Y
. Then, by Definition
2.3
,
X\subset Y
and there is
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
X^{\prime}\vdash Y
.
By the construction of
\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\cdot\right)
in Definition
3.2
, there is
Y^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}\!\mapsto_{\mu}\!Y^{\prime}
and
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
. Thus,
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\subset Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}\!\mapsto_{\mu}\!Y^{\prime}
. Then, as
\mapsto_{\mu}
is dense, also
X\!\mapsto_{\mu}\!Y
.

Next, we show that there is a natural way for—PESs, BESs, EBESs, and DESs—to derive a transition relation from the subset relation between posets.

{defi}

Let
\mu
be a rPES, BES, EBES, or DES with event set
E
.

We define the transition relation
{\mapsto}\subseteq\text{P}\!\left(E\right)^{2}
as follows:

X\!\mapsto\!Y
if there are two posets
\left(X,\leq_{1}\right)
and
\left(Y,\leq_{2}\right)
of
\mu
such that
X\subset Y
and
{\leq_{1}}\subseteq{\leq_{2}}
.

By construction, the above-defined transition relation is strict and dense as required in Definition
3.2
.
Accordingly, we associate each rPES, BES, EBES and DES with a corresponding transition equivalent RCES using the Definitions
3.2
and
3.2
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
be either of type rPES, BES, EBES, or DES, respectively.

Then,
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\mu_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu_{2}
.

Proof 3.3
.

Assume
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\mu_{2}
. Then,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
have the same posets and thus the same subset relations between these posets. Then, by Definition
3.2
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are associated with the same transition relation.
Immediately, we get
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu_{2}
.

Assume
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu_{2}
. Then,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
have the same transitions between sets of configurations. By Definition
2.3
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
then have the same configurations and traces. As shown in
[
17
]
, this implies that
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\mu_{2}
for all ESs without causal ambiguity (i.e., for rPESs, BESs, EBESs, DESs, and for DESs with posets based on early causality).

If posets are based on liberal, bundle satisfaction, minimal, or late causality, then the implication
\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\mu_{2}\implies\mu_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu_{2}
still holds, because its proof does not depend on the kind of posets that are used.

4.
Shrinking Causality

Now, we add a new relation that represents the removal of causal dependencies as a ternary relation between events
{\vartriangleleft}\subseteq E^{3}
. For instance,
\left(d,c,t\right)\in{\vartriangleleft}
, also written as
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
, models that the cause
c
is dropped from the set of causal predecessors of the target
t
by the occurrence of the so-called
dropper
event
d
. By definition, we ensure
d\notin\{c,t\}
; this is different w.r.t. the version in
[
2
]
.

The dropping is visualized in Figure
5
by an arrow with an empty head from the dependency
c\!\to\!t
to its dropper
d
.
We add this relation to rPESs and denote the result as
shrinking-causality
event structures.

{defi}

A
Shrinking Causality Event Structure (SES)
is a pair
\sigma=\left(\pi,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
, where
\pi=\left(E,\#,\to\right)
is a rPES and
{\vartriangleleft}\subseteq E^{3}
is the
shrinking causality
relation such that, for all
c,d,t\in E
,
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
implies
c\!\to\!t
and
d\notin\{c,t\}
.

Sometimes we flatten
\left(\pi,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
into
\left(E,\#,\rightarrow,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
.
For
m\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
, we call
m
the
modifier
,
t
the
target
, and
c
the
cause
. We denote the set of all modifiers dropping
c\rightarrow t
by
\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
.
We refer to the set of
dropped causes
of an event w.r.t. a specific history by the function
\operatorname{dc}:\text{P}\!\left(E\right)\times E\to\text{P}\!\left(E\right)
defined as:
\operatorname{dc}\!\left(H,e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists d\in H\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\right\}
.
We refer to the
initial causes
of an event by the function
\operatorname{ic}:E\to\text{P}\!\left(E\right)
such that:
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\!\to\!e\right\}
.

The semantics of a SES can be defined based on posets similar to BESs, EBESs, and DESs, or based on a transition relation similar to RCESs. We consider both.

{defi}

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES.

(1)

A
trace
of
\sigma
is a sequence of distinct events
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
with
\overline{t}\subseteq E
such that

•

\forall 1\leq i,j\leq n\,.\;\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
and

•

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
.

Then,
C\subseteq E
is a
trace-based configuration
of
\sigma
, if there is a trace
t
with
C=\overline{t}
.

\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
denotes the set of trace-based configurations, and
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
the set of traces of
\sigma
.

(2)

Let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and
1\leq i\leq n
.
A set
U
is a cause of
e_{i}
in
t
if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists 1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
,

•

\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq U
,
and

•

U
is the earliest set satisfying the previous two conditions.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{s}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for
t
.

(3)

Let
X,Y\subseteq E
.
Then,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
iff

•

X\subset Y
,

•

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\,.\;\neg(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime})
, and

•

\forall e\in Y{\setminus}X\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)\subseteq X
.

(4)

The set of all configurations of
\sigma
is

\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right):=\left\{X\subseteq E\mid\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}^{*}\!X\land X\text{ is finite}\right\}
,

where
\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}^{*}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}
.

(5)

For
X\subseteq Y\subseteq E
, we define the set of dropped dependencies as

\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}:=\left\{(c,t)\mid\exists d\in Y\setminus X\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]\right\}.

c
t
d

Figure 5.
Dropper visualization in SESs.

We use the Definitions
2.2
and
2.3
to obtain
\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}
and
\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}
for SESs.
The combination of initial and dropped causes ensures that for each
e_{i}\in\overline{t}
, all its initial causes are either preceding
e_{i}
or dropped by other events preceding
e_{i}
. Note that, as for DESs, we concentrate on early causality.
We consider the reachable and finite configurations w.r.t.
\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}
as well as configurations based on the traces. Note that both definitions coincide. (See Lemma
40
)

4.1.
An Alternative Transition Definition

In the following, we provide an alternative transition definition for SESs. Thereafter, we prove the equivalence of both definitions.

{defi}

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to_{\emptyset},{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES and

X,Y\subseteq E
.

Then,
(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})
if

(1)

X\subset Y
,

(2)

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\,.\;\neg(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime})
,

(3)

(a)

\forall e\in Y{\setminus}X\,.\;\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}\subseteq X
, and

(b)

{\to_{Y}}:={{\to_{X}}{\setminus}\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}
.

This definition stresses the
state
of the causality relation due to dynamic shrinking: all of the current causality information is explicitly accessible, instead of hidden in the
\operatorname{dc}
function. In order to prove the equivalence of both transition definitions, the following lemma provides an explicit, non-recursive definition of the current state of the causality relation.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to_{\emptyset},{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES and
(X,\to_{X})
be a state of
\sigma
.

Then,
{\to_{X}}={{\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus{\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}}}
.

Proof 4.1
.

Let
\emptyset=:C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{n-1},C_{n}:=X
be such that

(C_{i},\to_{C_{i}})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(C_{i+1},\to_{C_{i+1}})
for all
1\leq{i}<{n}
.
By Definition
4.1
, we get

{\to_{X}}=(\dots(\to_{\emptyset}\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{C_{0},C_{1}})\setminus\dots)\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{C_{n-1},X}
, and
by basic set theory and Definition of
4
(5),
we have
{\to_{X}}={\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}
.

Now we prove that both transition definitions of SESs coincide.

Theorem 8
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to_{\emptyset},{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES and
X,Y\subseteq E
. Then

X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y\iff(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y}).

Proof 4.2
.

Since the first conditions in the transition definitions of the Definitions
4
and
4.1
coincide, we show
\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;(\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\})
. This follows from definitions of
\operatorname{dc}
and
\operatorname{ic}
and Lemma
7
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\right\}\setminus\left\{c\mid\exists d\in X\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\wedge\,e^{\prime}\notin\left\{c\mid\exists d\in X\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\wedge\,(e^{\prime},e)\notin\left\{(c,e)\mid\exists d\in X\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus\left\{(c,t)\mid\exists d\in X\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}

By Lemma
7
, we have
{\to_{Y}}={{\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus{\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,Y}}}
and
{\to_{X}}={{\to_{\emptyset}}\setminus{\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}}}
. We can split
\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,Y}
into
\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}
and
\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}
and get
{\to_{Y}}={\to_{X}}\setminus{\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}
. Thus,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y\iff(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})
.

4.2.
SESs versus DESs

We show that SESs are as expressive as DESs
by the definition of mutual encodings that result in structures with equivalent behaviors.

Consider the shrinking causality
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
. It models the case that initially
t
causally depends on
c
, which can be dropped by the occurrence of
d
. Thus, for
t
to be enabled, either
c
occurs or
d
does. This is a disjunctive causality as modeled by DESs. In fact,
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
corresponds to the bundle
\left\{c,d\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!t
. We prove that we can map each SES into a DES with the same behavior and vice versa.
To translate a SES into a DES, we create a bundle for each initial causal dependence and add all its droppers to the bundle set.

{defi}

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES.

Then,
\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right):=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
, where
S\!\rightarrowtail\!y
iff

•

S\subseteq E
,

•

y\in E
, and

•

\exists x\in E\,.\;x\!\to\!y\land S=(\left\{x\right\}\cup\vartriangleleft\![x\rightarrow y])
.

We use Definition
4.2
to show that for each SES there is a DES with exactly the same traces and configurations.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\sigma
be a SES.
Then,
\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)
is a DES.

Proof 4.3
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES. By Definitions
4
and
2.1
,
\#\subseteq E^{2}
is irreflexive and symmetric. Hence, by Definitions
2.2
and
4.2
,
\delta=\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)
is a DES.

Lemma 10
.

Let
\sigma
be a SES.
Then,

\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)\right)
and

\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)\right)
.

Proof 4.4
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES.
Let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
.

By Definition
4
,
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
\overline{t}\subseteq E
and
\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
and also it holds
(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right))\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.
Since
\operatorname{dc}\!\left(H,e\right)=\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists d\in H\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\}
and
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\!\to\!e\right\}
, we have
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
iff
\forall e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e^{\prime}\!\to\!e_{i}\implies e^{\prime}\in\overline{t_{i-1}}\lor\exists d\in\overline{t_{i-1}}\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
By Definition
4.2
, then
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
\overline{t}\subseteq E
,
\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
, and
X\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}\implies\overline{t_{i-1}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
and all
X\subseteq E
.
Hence, by the definition of traces in §
2.2
,
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
, i.e.
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
.

Because of
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
(cf. Lemma
40
), §
2.2
, and Definition
4
, then also
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

The encoding also preserve posets.

Theorem 11
.

Let
\sigma
be a SES.
Then,
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Proof 4.5
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES.
By Lemmas
9
and
10
,
\delta=\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
is a DES such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)
.
Let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and the bundles
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
. For
U
to be a cause for
e_{i}
, Definition
4
requires
(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right))\subseteq U
. Since
\operatorname{dc}\!\left(H,e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists d\in H\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\right\}
and
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)=\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\!\to\!e\}
, this condition holds iff the condition
e^{\prime}\!\to\!e_{i}\implies e^{\prime}\in U\lor\exists d\in U\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]
holds for all
e^{\prime}\in E
.
By Definition
4.2
, then
(\forall 1\leq k\leq n\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset)\iff((\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right))\subseteq U)
.
So, by Definitions
2.2
and
4
,
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\delta
.

In the opposite direction, we map each DES into a set of similar SESs such that each SES in this set has the same behavior as the DES.
Intuitively, we have to choose an initial dependency for each bundle and to translate the remainder of the bundle set into droppers for that dependency. Unfortunately, the bundles that point to the same event are not necessarily disjoint. Consider for example
\left\{a,b\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!e
and
\left\{b,c\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!e
. If we choose
b\!\to\!e
as initial dependency for both bundles to be dropped as
a\!\vartriangleleft\![b\rightarrow e]
and
c\!\vartriangleleft\![b\rightarrow e]
, then
\left\{a,e\right\}
is a configuration of the resulting SES but not of the original DES. So, we have to ensure that we choose distinct events as initial causes for all bundles pointing to the same event.
Therefore, for each bundle
X_{i}\!\rightarrowtail\!e
, we choose a fresh event
x_{i}
as initial cause
x_{i}\!\to\!e
, make it impossible by a self-loop
x_{i}\!\to\!x_{i}
, and add all events
d
of the bundle
X_{i}
as droppers
d\!\vartriangleleft\![x_{i}\rightarrow e]
.
Note that, in order to translate a DES into a SES, we have to introduce additional events, i.e., it is not always possible to translate a DES into a SES without additional impossible events (cf. Lemma
42
).

{defi}

Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES,

\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
an enumeration of its bundles, and

\left\{x_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
a set of fresh events, i.e.
\left\{x_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}\cap E=\emptyset
.
Then,
\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right):=\left(E^{\prime},\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
with

•

E^{\prime}:=E\cup\left\{x_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
,

•

{\to}:=\left\{x_{i}\!\to\!e\mid X_{i}\!\rightarrowtail\!e\right\}\cup\left\{x_{i}\!\to\!x_{i}\mid i\in I\right\}
, and

•

{\vartriangleleft}:=\left\{d\!\vartriangleleft\![x_{i}\rightarrow e]\mid d\in X_{i}\land X_{i}\!\rightarrowtail\!e\right\}
.

Because the
x_{i}
are fresh, there are no droppers for the self-loops
x_{i}\!\to\!x_{i}
in
\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)
. So the encoding ensures that all events in
\left\{x_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
remain impossible forever in the resulting SES. In fact, we show that the DES and its encoding have the very same traces and configurations.

Lemma 12
.

For each DES
\delta
there is a SES
\sigma
, namely
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)
, such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Proof 4.6
.

Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES. By Definition
2.2
,
\#\subseteq E^{2}
is irreflexive and symmetric. Hence, by the Definitions
4
,
2.1
, and
4.2
,
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)=\left(E^{\prime},\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
is a SES.

Let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
. Then, by Definition
4
,
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
\overline{t}\subseteq E
and
\neg(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j})
and also
(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right))\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Note that we have
\overline{t}\subseteq E
instead of
\overline{t}\subseteq E^{\prime}
, because all events in
t
have to be distinct and for all events in
E^{\prime}\setminus E
there is an initial self-loop but no dropper.
Since
\operatorname{dc}\!\left(H,e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists d\in H\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\right\}
and
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\!\to\!e\right\}
,
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)

\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
iff
\forall e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e^{\prime}\!\to\!e_{i}\implies e^{\prime}\in\overline{t_{i-1}}\lor\exists d\in\overline{t_{i-1}}\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
By Definition
4.2
, then
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
\overline{t}\subseteq E
,
\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
, and
X\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}\implies\overline{t_{i-1}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
and all
X\subseteq E
.
Hence, by the Definition of traces in §
2.2
,
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
iff
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
, i.e.
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
.

Because of
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
(cf. Lemma
40
), the Definition of
configurations in §
2.2
, and Definition
4
, then
also
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Moreover the DES and its encoding have exactly the same posets.

Theorem 13
.

For each DES
\delta
there is a SES
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)
such that
\delta\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma
.

Proof 4.7
.

Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES. By Lemma
12
,
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
is a SES such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and the bundles
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.
For
U
to be a cause for
e_{i}
Definition
4
requires
(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right))\subseteq U
.
Since
\operatorname{dc}\!\left(H,e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists d\in H\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\right\}
and
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)=\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\!\to\!e\}
, this condition holds iff the condition
e^{\prime}\!\to\!e_{i}\implies e^{\prime}\in U\lor\exists d\in U\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]
holds for all
e^{\prime}\in E
.
By Definition
4.2
, then
(\forall 1\leq k\leq n\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset)
iff
\left(\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq U\right)
.
So, by the Definitions
2.2
and
4
,
\delta\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma
.

Thus SESs and DESs have the same expressive power.

Theorem 14
.

SESs are as expressive as DESs.

Proof 4.8
.

By the Theorems
11
and
13
.

Another ES with disjunctive causality are the Stable Event Structures (StESs)
[
25
]
.
Katoen
[
14
]
proves that StESs are strictly less expressive than DESs. Note that for this particular expressiveness result it does not matter whether the definition of DESs is based on BESs (as here and in
[
17
]
) or EBESs (as in
[
14
]
).
Then,
[
8
]
introduces the Flow Event Structures (FESs) and proves that PESs are strictly less expressive than FESs, and that FESs are strictly less expressive than StESs.
Since we restrict our attention to finite configurations and because of Definition
2.1
, PESs and rPESs have the same expressive power.
Finally, that BESs are strictly less expressive than FESs was shown in
[
6
]
and (more directly) in
[
16
]
.
With the above theorem we conclude that SESs are strictly more expressive than StESs, FESs, BESs, PESs and rPESs.

Corollary 15
.

SESs are strictly more expressive than rPESs, BESs, FESs, and StESs.

In
[
17
]
, the authors prove that for DESs equivalence w.r.t. posets based on early causality coincides with trace equivalence. Since SESs are as expressive as DESs w.r.t. families of posets based on early causality, the same correspondence holds for SESs.

Corollary 16
.

Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
be two SESs. Then
\sigma_{1}\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma_{2}
iff
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma_{1}\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma_{2}\right)
.

In Appendix
B
, we show that each SES and its encoding as well as each DES and its encoding have the same set of posets considering liberal, minimal, and late causality.
Thus, the concepts of SESs and DESs are not only behaviorally equivalent but—except for the additional impossible events—also structurally closely related.

Note that
\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}
,
\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}
, and trace equivalence coincide on SES.

Theorem 17
.

Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be two SESs.
Then
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma^{\prime}
iff
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\sigma^{\prime}
iff
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof 4.9
.

By Corollary
16
,
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma^{\prime}
iff
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
.

If
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)\neq\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
then, because of
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
(cf. Lemma
40
) and the Definitions
4
and
4
,
\sigma\not\simeq_{\operatorname{p}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma\not\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\sigma^{\prime}
. Hence assume
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
.
Note that, by Definition
4
and
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
(cf. Lemma
40
), for all
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
there is a trace
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
such that
\overline{t}=C
. Moreover, for every trace
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
except the empty trace there is a sub-trace
t^{\prime}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and a sequence of events
e_{1}\cdots e_{m}
such that
t=t^{\prime}e_{1}\cdots e_{m}
and
\forall e\in\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\right\}\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t^{\prime}},e\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t^{\prime}}
.
Thus, by Lemma
41
,
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
iff
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\sigma^{\prime}
.

4.3.
SESs versus EBESs

a
b
c
(\beta_{\gamma})
a
b
c
(\sigma_{\xi})
e
f
(\xi_{\sigma})
b

(\gamma_{\sigma})
b
