(\gamma_{\xi})

Figure 6.
Counterexamples.

SESs allow us to model disjunctive causality. As an example, consider the dropping of a causality as in
\sigma_{\xi}
of Figure
6
(or Figure
5
). Such a disjunctive causality is not possible in EBESs.
On the other hand, the asymmetric conflict of an EBES cannot be modeled with a SES. As an example, consider
\xi_{\sigma}
of Figure
6
, where
f
cannot precede
e
.
Hence, EBESs and SESs are incomparable.

Theorem 18
.

SESs and EBESs are incomparable.

Proof 4.10
.

Let
\sigma_{\xi}=\left(\left\{a,b,c\right\},\emptyset,\left\{a\!\to\!b\right\},\left\{c\!\vartriangleleft\![a\rightarrow b]\right\}\right)
be the SES that is depicted in Figure
6
.
Assume there is some EBES
\xi=\left(E,\leadsto,\rightarrowtail\right)
such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi\right)
.
By Definition
4
,
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)=\left\{\epsilon,a,c,ab,ac,ca,cb,abc,acb,cab,cba\right\}
, i.e.
b
cannot occur first.
By Definition
2.2
, a disabling
x\!\leadsto\!y
implies that
y
can never precedes
x
.
Thus, we have
\leadsto\cap\left\{a,b,c\right\}^{2}=\emptyset
, because within
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)
each pair of events of
\left\{a,b,c\right\}
occur in any order.
Similarly, we have
\rightarrowtail\cap\{X\!\rightarrowtail\!e\mid e\in\left\{a,b,c\right\}\land X\cap\left\{a,b,c\right\}=\emptyset\}=\emptyset
, because
x\!\rightarrowtail\!y
implies that
x
always has to precede
y
.
Moreover, by Definition
2.2
, adding impossible events as causes or using them within the disabling relation does not influence the set of traces.
Thus, there is no EBES
\xi
with the same traces as
\sigma_{\xi}
. By Definition
2.2
and the definition of posets in EBESs, there is then no EBES
\xi
with the same configurations or posets as
\sigma_{\xi}
.

Let
\xi_{\sigma}=\left(\left\{e,f\right\},\left\{e\!\leadsto\!f\right\},\emptyset\right)
be the EBES that is depicted in Figure
6
.
Assume that there is some SES
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi_{\sigma}\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.
According to §
2.2
, we have
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi_{\sigma}\right)=\left\{\epsilon,e,f,ef\right\}
.
By Definition
4
and because of the traces
e
and
f
, there are no initial causes for
e
and f, i.e.
\to\cap\left\{x\!\to\!y\mid y\in\left\{e,f\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
.
Moreover,
\#\cap\left\{e,f\right\}^{2}=\emptyset
, because of the trace
ef
and because conflicts cannot be dropped.
Thus
fe\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
but
fe\notin\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi_{\sigma}\right)
, i.e. there is no SES
\sigma
with the same traces as
\xi_{\sigma}
.
Then, by the Definitions
4
and
4
, there is no SES
\sigma
with the same configurations or families of posets as
\xi_{\sigma}
.

4.4.
SESs versus RCESs

SESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs. Each SES can be translated into a transition-equivalent RCES.

Lemma 19
.

For each SES
\sigma
there is a RCES
\rho
such that
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho
.

Proof 4.11
.

By the Definitions
4
and
4
,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
implies
X\subseteq Y
for all
X,Y\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Assume that
X\subseteq X^{\prime}\subseteq Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
.
Then, by Definition
4
,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
implies that
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)
and
\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)\subseteq X
.
Then
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
implies that
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X^{\prime},e\right)\right)\subseteq\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)
. Then, because of
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
,
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)
and
\forall e\in Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X^{\prime},e\right)\right)\subseteq X^{\prime}
.
By Definition
4
, we then have
X^{\prime}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y^{\prime}
.

Thus,
\sigma
satisfies the conditions of Definition
3.2
. Then by Lemma
5
,
\rho=\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\sigma\right)
is a RCES such that
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho
.

On the other hand, there are RCESs that cannot be translated into a transition-equivalent SES.
As a counterexample, we use the RCES
\rho_{\sigma}=(E,{\vdash})
, where
E=\{e,f\}
and
{\vdash}=\{(\emptyset,\{e\}),(\emptyset,\{f\}),(\{f\},\{e,f\})\}
, which captures disabling in an EBES.

Lemma 20
.

There is no transition-equivalent SES to the RCES
\rho_{\sigma}=(E,{\vdash})
, where
E=\{e,f\}
and
{\vdash}=\{(\emptyset,\{e\}),(\emptyset,\{f\}),(\{f\},\{e,f\})\}
.

Proof 4.12
.

Assume a SES
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
such that
\sigma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho_{\sigma}
. Then
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\sigma}\right)
.
By Definition
4
and
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
(cf. Lemma
40
) and because of the configuration
\left\{e,f\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\sigma}\right)
, the events
e
and
f
cannot be in conflict with each other, i.e.
\#\cap\left\{e,f\right\}^{2}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, because of the configurations
\left\{e\right\},\left\{f\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\sigma}\right)
, there are no initial causes for
e
and
f
, i.e.
\to\cap\left\{x\!\to\!y\mid y\in\left\{e,f\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
.
Note that the relation
{\vartriangleleft}
cannot disable events.
Thus, we have
\forall a,b\in\left\{e,f\right\}\,.\;\neg\left(a\mathrel{\#}b\right)
and
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\left\{f\right\},e\right)\right)=\emptyset\subseteq\left\{f\right\}
.
But then, by Definition
4
, we have
\left\{f\right\}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\left\{e,f\right\}
.
Since
\left\{f\right\}\mathrel{\mapsto_{1}}\left\{e,f\right\}
does not hold, this violates our assumption, i.e. there is no SES that is transition equivalent to
\rho_{\sigma}
.

Hence, SESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs.

Theorem 21
.

SESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs.

Proof 4.13
.

Follows from the Lemmata
20
and
19
.

5.
Growing Causality

b
a
(a)
a
b
(b)
a
b
c
(c)
a
b
c
(d)

Figure 7.
GESs modeling disabling, conflict, temporary disabling, and resolvable conflicts.

Like with SESs, we express our extension for growing causality on rPESs by a new relation: we use
{\blacktriangleright}\subseteq E^{3}
, where
\left(a,c,t\right)\in{\blacktriangleright}
, also denoted as
a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]
, models the fact that the occurrence of the so-called
adder

a
adds
c
as a cause for the target
t
. Thus,
a
is a condition for the causal dependency
c\!\to\!t
. By definition, we will ensure that
a\notin\{c,t\}
, which is a difference to the version in
[
2
]
.
The relation
{\blacktriangleright}
is visualized in the example of Figure
7
(c) by an arrow with a filled head from the modifier
c
to the added dependency
a\!\to\!b
; to denote that this dependency does not initially exist, it is depicted as a dotted arrow. (In this example, there is an additional causality
c\!\to\!a
.)

a
b
c
a
b
c
e_{\text{imp}}

Figure 8.
A rPES with binary conflict (and no inherited conflict) and a simulating GES.

In comparison to a rPES, we can model a binary conflict between
a
and
c
by a mutual disabling, and a disabling of an event by an addition of an impossible cause: assume a fresh impossible event
e_{\text{imp}}
with
e_{\text{imp}}\!\to\!e_{\text{imp}}
and the mutual disabling
b\!\blacktriangleright\![e_{\text{imp}}\rightarrow a]
and
a\!\blacktriangleright\![e_{\text{imp}}\rightarrow b]
(see Figure
8
). Thus, we omit the conflict relation in the Definition of GESs.

{defi}

A
Growing Causality Event Structure (GES)
is a triple
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
, where
E
is a set of events,
{\to}\subseteq{E^{2}}
the initial causality relation, and
{\blacktriangleright}\subseteq E^{3}
is the
growing causality
relation such that, for all
a,c,t\in E
,
a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]
implies
\lnot(c\rightarrow t)
and
a\notin\{c,t\}
.

We refer to the causes added to an event w.r.t. a specific history by the function
\operatorname{ac}:\text{P}\!\left(E\right)\times E\to\text{P}\!\left(E\right)
, defined as
\operatorname{ac}\!\left(H,e\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid\exists a\in H\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e]\right\}
, and to the initial causality by the function
\operatorname{ic}
as defined in §
4
.
Similar to the RCESs the behavior of a GES can be defined by a transition relation.

{defi}

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
be a GES.

•

A
trace
of
\gamma
is a sequence of distinct events
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
with
\overline{t}\subseteq E
such that

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}.

Then
C\subseteq E
is a
trace-based configuration
of
\gamma
if there is a trace
t
such that
C=\overline{t}
. The set of traces of
\gamma
is denoted by
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma\right)
and the set of its trace-based configurations is denoted by
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)
.

•

Let
X,Y\subseteq E
. Then
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y
iff

(1)

X\subset Y

(2)

\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X,e\right))\subseteq X

(3)

\forall t,a\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X
.

•

The set of all configurations of
\gamma
is
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\left\{X\subseteq E\mid\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}^{*}\!X\land X\text{ is finite }\right\}
, where
\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}^{*}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}
.

•

For
X\subseteq Y\subseteq E
we define the set of added dependencies as

\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}:=\left\{(c,t)\mid\exists a\in Y\setminus X\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\right\}.

The last condition in the transition definition prevents the concurrent occurrence of a target and its modifier since they are not independent. The exception is when the cause has already occurred; in that case, the modifier does not change the target’s predecessors.
Again, we consider the reachable and finite configurations, and show in the Appendix (cf. Lemma
44
) that the definitions of reachable and trace-based configurations coincide.
We use Definition
2.3
to obtain
\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}
for GESs and consider two GESs as equally expressive if they are transition equivalent.

5.1.
An Alternative Transition Definition

In the following, we provide an alternative transition definition for GESs, similar to the SESs in Definition
4.1
. As before, this definition stresses the
state
of the causal dependencies, but this time due to its dynamic growth. Thereafter, we prove the equivalence of both definitions.

{defi}

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to_{\emptyset},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
be a GES,
and
X,Y\subseteq E
.

Then
(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})
, if

(1)

X\subset Y
,

(2)

(a)

\forall e\in Y{\setminus}X\,.\;\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}\subseteq X
,

(b)

{\to_{Y}}={\to_{X}}\cup{\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}
, and

(3)

\forall t,a\in Y{\setminus}X\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X
.

Here each state explicitly shows all causality information (without the
\operatorname{ac}
function) and the growing of the causality relation is explicitly represented by the causal state
\to_{X}
.

The following Lemma provides an explicit, non-recursive definition of causal states.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to_{\emptyset},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
be a GES and
(X,\to_{X})
be a state of
\gamma
.

Then
{\to_{X}}={\to_{\emptyset}\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}}
.

Proof 5.1
.

Let
\emptyset=:C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{n-1},C_{n}:=X
be a configuration sequence such that we get
(C_{0},\to_{\emptyset})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(C_{1},\to_{C1})\dots(C_{i},\to_{C_{i}})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(C_{i+1},\to_{C_{i+1}})\dots(C_{n-1},\to_{C_{n-1}})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s^{\prime}}}\!(X,\to_{X})
.

By Definition
5.1
, we have
\to_{X}=(\dots(\to_{\emptyset}\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{C_{0},C_{1}})\cup\dots)\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{C_{n-1},X}
, and with basic set theory, and the Definition of
\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}
in
5
, we get
{\to_{X}}={\to_{\emptyset}\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}}
.

Now we can prove that both transition definitions of GESs coincide.

Theorem 23
.

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to_{\emptyset},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
be a GES and
X,Y\subseteq E
. Then

X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y\iff(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y}).

Proof 5.2
.

Since all but condition
(2)
in the transition definitions in
5
and
5.1
coincide, we just show
\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;(\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)=\left\{e^{\prime}\mid{(e^{\prime},e)}\in{\to_{X}}\right\})
. This follows from definitions of
\operatorname{ac}
and
\operatorname{ic}
and Lemma
22
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X,e\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\right\}\cup\left\{c\mid\exists a\in X\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\vee\,e^{\prime}\in\left\{c\mid\exists a\in X\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\vee\,(e^{\prime},e)\in\left\{(c,e)\mid\exists a\in X\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow e]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\cup\left\{(c,t)\mid\exists a\in X\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{\emptyset}}\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\emptyset,X}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}

As
{\to_{Y}}={\to_{X}\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}
by Lemma
22
,
we get
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y\iff(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})
.

5.2.
Expressive Power

Disabling as defined in EBESs or the asymmetric event structure of
[
4
]
can be modeled by
{\blacktriangleright}
. For example,
b\!\leadsto\!a
can be modeled by
b\!\blacktriangleright\![a\rightarrow a]
as depicted in Figure
7
(a). Analogously, conflicts can be modeled by
{\blacktriangleright}
through mutual disabling, as depicted in Figure
7
(b). However, if we later combine shrinking and growing causality ESs in §
6
, another dropper
d
can resolve the disabling.

Let us reconsider the example GES in Figure
7
(c).
Initially,
b
is enabled and
a
is disabled.
The occurrence of
c
, which (enables
a
and) disables
b
by adding
a
as a cause with
c\!\blacktriangleright\![a\rightarrow b]
. By this,
b
may be
temporarily disabled
by an occurrence of
c
until
a
occurs and
re-enables

b
.
In
inhibitor event structures

[
3
]
, another kind of disabling can be expressed, where an event
b
can be disabled by another event
c
until an event out of a
set

X
(instead of only a single event
a
, as above) occurs. This behavior—which may be called
disjunctive re-enabling
—cannot be modeled in GESs, but in DCESs (cf. §
6
).

Also resolvable conflicts can be modeled by a GES. For example the GES in Figure
7
(d) with
a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow b]
and
b\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow a]
models a conflict between
a
and
b
that can be resolved by
c
. Note that this example depends on the idea that a modifier and its target cannot occur concurrently (cf. Definition
5
). Note also that resolvable conflicts are a reason why families of configurations are not sufficient to describe the semantics of GESs or RCESs.

5.3.
GESs versus PESs

The GESs are strictly more expressive than rPES and PESs, since they are in essence a generalization of the rPES (when the conflict relation is encoded as mutual disabling, i.e. by mutual adding of an impossible cause) and they can express a disabling of an event (as in Fig.
7
(b)).

Lemma 24
.

The GESs are strictly more expressive than the rPESs and PESs.

Proof 5.3
.

Let
\pi=\left(E,\#,\to\right)
be a rPES. Then, its embedding
\gamma_{\pi}
into a GES is given by
\gamma_{\pi}:=(E\cup\{e_{\text{imp}}\},\to\cup\{(e_{\text{imp}},e_{\text{imp}})\},\{(a,e_{\text{imp}},b),(b,e_{\text{imp}},a)\mid(a,b)\in\#)\}
for a fresh (and impossible) event
e_{\text{imp}}
.

Let
C
be a configuration in
\pi
. By Definition
2.1
, we have that
C
is downward-closed, i.e.
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e\!\to\!e^{\prime}\land e^{\prime}\in C\implies e\in C
, conflict-free, i.e.
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in C\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)
, and
{\to}\cap{C^{2}}
is free of cycles. Let further be
C=\left\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\right\}
such that also all
C_{j}:=\left\{e_{i}\mid i\leq j\right\}
are configurations of
\pi
(this is always possible since
C
is conflict-free and
{\to}\cap{C^{2}}
is free of cycles).
Now, we have
C_{i}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!C_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq n{-}1
, as all three conditions in Definition
5
are met.
The first condition is satisfied, as
C_{i}\subset C_{i+1}
by construction.
The second condition is satisfied, because the only added causality is the disabling of conflicting events and thus does not affect events in
C
, and then it coincides with the downward-closure.
The third condition just prevents, w.r.t. our encoding, the concurrent occurrence of conflicting events.
Let on the other hand
t=e_{1},\dots e_{n}
be a trace in
\gamma_{\pi}
. By Definition
5
, we have

\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}.

We show that all
C_{j}:=\left\{e_{i}\mid i\leq j\right\}
with
j\leq n
are conflict-free and downward-closed in
\pi
and
{\to}\cap{C^{2}}
is free of cycles: Assume that
C_{j}
is the smallest not conflict-free set then it contains
e_{i}
and
e_{j}
with
e_{i}\#e_{j}
for some
i<j
. But since they are in conflict we have
e_{i}\!\blacktriangleright\![e_{\text{imp}}\rightarrow e_{j}]\in{\blacktriangleright}
, where
e_{\text{imp}}
is impossible. This contradicts

\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{j}\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{j-1}},e_{j}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{j-1}}

because
e_{\text{imp}}
is initially impossible and thus
e_{\text{imp}}\not\in\overline{t_{i-1}}
. If some
C_{j}
would not be downward-closed, the
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{j}\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{j-1}}
would not hold. Let
{\to}\cap{C_{j}^{2}}
be the minimal not cycle-free relation, then
e_{j}
must have introduced a cycle and thus be a cause of some
e_{i}
with
i<j
, but then
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
would not hold. Therefore, we have that
\gamma_{\pi}
is a proper encoding of
\pi
.

Now consider the GES
\gamma=(\left\{a,b\right\},\emptyset,\left\{(b,a,a)\right\})
(as in Figure
7
(b)). By Definition
5
,
b
and
ab
are possible traces in
\gamma
, but
ba
is forbidden. If there was an equivalent rPES
\pi_{\gamma}
, the trace
b
would imply that
b
does not depend on
a
. The trace
ab
would imply that
\neg(a\#b)
, and therefore
ba
would also be possible in
\pi_{\gamma}
, contradicting the equivalence assumption.

Combining the two results, we get that GESs are strictly more expressive than rPESs. As we restrict our attention to finite configurations and with Definition
2.1
, rPESs and PESs have the same expressive power. Hence, GESs are strictly more expressive than PESs.

5.4.
GESs versus EBESs

As shown in Figure
7
(a), GESs can model disabling. Nevertheless, EBESs and GESs are incomparable.

On the one hand, GESs cannot model the disjunction in the enabling relation that EBESs inherit from BESs. The BES
\beta_{\gamma}
of Figure
6
models a simple case of disjunction in the enabling relation.

Lemma 25
.

There is no configuration-equivalent GES to
\beta_{\gamma}
of Figure
6
.

Proof 5.4
.

Assume a GES
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
such that
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta_{\gamma}\right)
. According to §
2.2
,
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta_{\gamma}\right)=\left\{\emptyset,\left\{a\right\},\left\{b\right\},\left\{a,c\right\},\left\{b,c\right\}\right\}
. Because
\left\{c\right\}\notin\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta_{\gamma}\right)
,
\left\{a,c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta_{\gamma}\right)
, and by Definition
5
and
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
(cf. Lemma
44
),
a
has to be an initial cause of
c
in
\gamma
, i.e.
a\!\to\!c
.
But then, by Definition
5
and
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
(cf. Lemma
44
),
\left\{b,c\right\}\notin\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
although
\left\{b,c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\beta_{\gamma}\right)
. This violates our assumption, i.e. no GES can be configuration equivalent to
\beta_{\gamma}
.

On the other hand, EBESs cannot model conditional causal dependencies as visualized by the GES
\gamma_{\xi}
of Figure
6
.

Lemma 26
.

There is no trace-equivalent EBES to
\gamma_{\xi}
of Figure
6
.

Proof 5.5
.

Assume a EBES
\xi=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\xi}\right)
. By Definition
5
, we have
a,c,ca,bac\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\xi}\right)
and
ac\notin\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\xi}\right)
. Because of
a,c\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\xi}\right)
and by Definition
2.2
,
a
and
c
have to be initially enabled in
\xi
, i.e.
\rightarrowtail\cap\left\{X\!\rightarrowtail\!y\mid y\in\left\{a,c\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
. Moreover, because of
ca,bac\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\xi}\right)
,
a
cannot disable
c
, i.e.
\neg\left(c\!\leadsto\!a\right)
. But then
ac\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\xi\right)
. This violates our assumption, i.e. there is no trace-equivalent EBES to
\gamma_{\xi}
.

Thus GESs are incomparable to BESs as well as EBESs.

Theorem 27
.

GESs are incomparable to BESs and EBESs.

Proof 5.6
.

By Lemma
25
, there is no GES that is configuration equivalent to the BES
\beta_{\gamma}
. Thus, no GES can have the same families of posets as the BES
\beta_{\gamma}
, because two BESs with different configurations cannot have the same families of posets (cf. §
2.2
). Moreover, by the Definitions
2.2
and
2.2
, each BES is also an EBES. Thus, no GES can have the same families of posets as the EBES
\beta_{\gamma}
.

By Lemma
26
, there is no EBES and thus also no BES that is trace equivalent to the GES
\gamma_{\xi}
. By Definition
5
, two GESs with different traces cannot have the same transition graphs. Thus no EBES or BES can be transition equivalent to
\gamma_{\xi}
.

5.5.
GESs versus SESs

GESs are also incomparable to SESs, because the addition of causes cannot be modeled by SESs. As a counterexample, we use the GES
\gamma_{\sigma}
of Figure
6
.

Lemma 28
.

There is no trace-equivalent SES to
\gamma_{\sigma}
of Figure
6
.

Proof 5.7
.

Assume a SES
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\sigma}\right)
. By Definition
5
,
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\sigma}\right)=\left\{\epsilon,a,b,ab\right\}
.
Because of the trace
ab\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\sigma}\right)
and by Definition
4
,
a
and
b
cannot be in conflict, i.e.
\neg(a\mathrel{\#}b)
and
\neg(b\mathrel{\#}a)
. Moreover, because of the traces
a,b\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\sigma}\right)
, there are no initial cases for
a
or
b
, i.e.
\to\cap\left\{x\!\to\!y\mid y\in\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
. Thus, by Definition
4
,
ba\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
but
ba\notin\operatorname{T}\!\left(\gamma_{\sigma}\right)
. This violates our assumption, i.e. no SES can be trace equivalent to
\gamma_{\sigma}
.

Then since BESs are incomparable to GESs, BESs are less expressive than DESs, and DESs are as expressive as SESs, we conclude that GESs and SESs are incomparable.

Theorem 29
.

GESs and SESs are incomparable.

Proof 5.8
.

By Lemma
28
, no SES is trace equivalent to the GES
\gamma_{\sigma}
. By Definition
5
, two GESs with different traces cannot have the same transition graphs. Thus, no SES is transition equivalent to the GES
\gamma_{\sigma}
.

By
[
16
]
, BESs are less expressive than EBESs and by
[
17
]
, BESs are less expressive than DESs. By Theorem
27
, BESs and GESs are incomparable and, by Theorem
14
, DESs are as expressive as SESs.
Thus, GESs and SESs are incomparable.

5.6.
GESs versus RCESs

As illustrated in Figure
7
(d), GESs can model resolvable conflicts. Nevertheless, they are strictly less expressive than RCESs. We show first that each GES can be translated by Definition
3.2
into a transition-equivalent RCES.

Lemma 30
.

For each GES
\gamma
there is an RCES
\rho
, namely
\rho=\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\gamma\right)
, such that
\gamma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho
.

Proof 5.9
.

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
. By Definition
5
,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y
implies
X\subseteq Y
.

Assume
X\subseteq X^{\prime}\subseteq Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
and
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y
.
By Definition
5
, then we have that
\forall e\in(Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime})\,.\;(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X,e\right))\subseteq X
, and
\forall t,m\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;m\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X
. Moreover, because
\forall t,m\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;m\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X
,
\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X,e\right)=\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X^{\prime},e\right)
for all
e\in Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}
.
As a consequence, we have that
\forall e\in\left(Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}\right).\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X^{\prime},e\right)\right)\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
\forall t,m\in Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;m\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X^{\prime}
. Thus, by Definition
5
,
X^{\prime}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!Y^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
5
,
\rho=\operatorname{rces}\!\left(\gamma\right)
is an RCES and
\gamma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho
.

On the other hand, there is no GES that is transition equivalent to the RCES
\rho_{\gamma}
in Figure
4
.
It models the case, where after
a
and
b
the event
c
becomes impossible, i.e. it models disabling by a set instead of a single event.

Lemma 31
.

There is no transition-equivalent GES to
\rho_{\gamma}
of Figure
4
.

Proof 5.10
.

Assume a GES
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
such that
\gamma\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho_{\gamma}
. Then
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
. By Definition
5
, and because of the configurations
\left\{a\right\},\left\{b\right\},\left\{c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
, there are no initial causes for
a
,
b
, or
c
, i.e.
{\to}\cap\left\{x\!\to\!y\mid y\in\left\{a,b,c\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
. Moreover, because of the configurations
\left\{a,c\right\},\left\{b,c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
, neither
a
nor
b
can add a cause to
c
. Thus, we have
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(c\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\left\{a,b\right\},c\right)\right)=\emptyset\subseteq\left\{a,b\right\}
. But then, by Definition
5
,
\left\{a,b\right\}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!\left\{a,b,c\right\}
. Since
\neg\left(\left\{a,b\right\}\mathrel{\mapsto_{\rho_{\gamma}}}\left\{a,b,c\right\}\right)
, this violates our assumption, i.e. there is no GES that is transition equivalent to
\rho_{\gamma}
.

Hence, GESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs.

Theorem 32
.

GESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs.

Proof 5.11
.

Follows from Lemma
30
and
31
.

6.
Dynamic Causality

We have investigated shrinking and growing causality separately. In this section, we combine them and examine the resulting expressive power. Note that we again omit the binary conflict relation from this definition, because it can be modeled by adders (cf. §
5
). Since we slightly changed the definitions for the shrinking and growing causality relations, there is a slight difference to the version in
[
2
]
.

{defi}

A
Dynamic Causality Event Structure (DCES)
is a quadruple
\Delta=
\left(E,\to,{\vartriangleleft},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
, where
E
is a set of events,
\to\;\subseteq E^{2}
the initial causality relation,
{\vartriangleleft}\subseteq E^{3}
is the shrinking causality relation, and
{\blacktriangleright}\subseteq E^{3}
is the growing causality relation such that for all
d,c,t,a\in E
:

(1)

d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]\land\nexists a\in E\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\Longrightarrow c\rightarrow t

(2)

d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]\Longrightarrow d\notin\{c,t\}

(3)

a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\land\nexists d\in E\,.\;d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]\Longrightarrow\lnot(c\rightarrow t)

(4)

a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\Longrightarrow a\notin\{c,t\}

(5)

a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\Longrightarrow\neg(d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t])
.

The Conditions
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
are a generalization of the Conditions in the Definitions
4
and
5
, respectively. If there are droppers and adders for the same causal dependency we do not specify whether this dependency is contained in
\to
, because the semantics depends on the order in which the droppers and adders occur. Condition
5
prevents that a modifier adds and drops the same cause for the same target.

c
t
a
d

Figure 9.
An order sensitive DCES.

The order of occurrence of droppers and adders determines the causes of an event. For example assume
a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]
and
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow t]
(as depicted in Figure
9
), then after
ad
,
t
does not depend on
c
, whereas after
da
,
t
depends on
c
. Thus, configurations like
\left\{a,d\right\}
are not expressive enough to represent the state of such a system.

Therefore, in a DCES, a state is a pair of a configuration
C
and the current causal state, i.e., the relation
\to_{C}
that contains the current causal dependencies.
Note that the initial state is the only configuration with an empty set of events; for other sets of events there can be different configuration.
The behavior of a DCES is defined by the transition relation between finite configurations.

{defi}

Let
\Delta=\left(E,\to_{in},{\vartriangleleft},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
be a DCES and
X,Y\subseteq E
.

Then

(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{d}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})
if:

(1)

X\subset Y

(2)

\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}\subseteq X

(3)

{\to_{Y}}={(\to_{X}\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y})\cup\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}

(4)

\forall(c,t)\in{\,\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L 10.23 5.91 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}\cap{\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 11.81 L -10.23 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{X,Y}}\,.\;(c\in X\vee t\in X)

(5)

\forall t,a\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\;a\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X
.

Condition
1
ensures the accumulation of events. Condition
2
ensures that only events that are enabled after
X
can take place in
Y
. Condition
3
ensures the correct update of the current causality relation with respect to this transition. Condition
4
prevents race conditions; it forbids the occurrence of an adder and a dropper of the same still relevant causal dependency within one transition. Condition
5
ensures that DCESs generalizes the GESs, it prevents the concurrent occurrence of an adder and its target since they are not independent. The one exception is when the cause has already occurred; in that case, the adder does not change the target’s predecessors.

{defi}

Let
\Delta=(E,\to_{in},{\vartriangleleft},{\blacktriangleright})
and
\Delta^{\prime}=(E^{\prime},\to_{in}^{\prime},{\vartriangleleft}^{\prime},{\blacktriangleright}^{\prime})
be two DCESs. We call them transition sequence equivalent if they allow for the same transition sequences with respect to the events and write
\Delta\!\simeq_{\operatorname{ts}}\!\Delta^{\prime}
, i.e. for any sequence
\emptyset=X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
with
X_{i}\subset X_{i+1}
and
X_{n}\in E\cap E^{\prime}
, we have current causality relations
\to_{X_{0}}=\to_{in},\to_{X_{1}},\dots,\to_{X_{n}}
such that
(X_{i},\to_{X_{i}})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{d}}\!(X_{i+1},\to_{X_{i+1}})
in
\Delta
, iff we have current causality relations
\to_{X_{0}}^{\prime}=\to_{in}^{\prime},\to_{X_{1}}^{\prime}\dots,\to_{X_{n}}^{\prime}
such that
(X_{i},\to_{X_{i}}^{\prime})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{d}}\!(X_{i+1},\to_{X_{i+1}^{\prime}})
in
\Delta^{\prime}
.

As visualized by the DCES in Figure
9
, the order of events may be relevant for the behavior of a DCES. Therefore, we use state transition equivalence to compare DCESs. When we compare a DCES to GESs, SESs, RCESs, and EBES, then the state will be unique with respect to the configuration (i.e. the current causality relation only depends on the configuration and not on the order), thus we can use the transition equivalence
\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}
from Definition
2.3
for those comparisons.

6.1.
DCESs versus RCESs

DCESs and RCESs are incomparable.

On the one hand, RCESs can express the disabling of an event
c
after a conjunction of events
a
and
b
as visualized by
\rho_{\gamma}
of Figure
4
, whereas DCESs can model only disabling after single events.

Lemma 33
.

There is no transition-equivalent DCES to
\rho_{\gamma}
of Figure
4
.

Proof 6.1
.

Assume
\Delta=\left(E,\to,{\vartriangleleft},{\blacktriangleright}\right)
such that
\Delta\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\rho_{\gamma}
. Then
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\Delta\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
.
By Definition
6
and because of the configurations
\left\{a\right\},\left\{b\right\},\left\{c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
, there are no initial causes for
a
,
b
, or
c
, i.e.
\to\cap\left\{x\!\to\!y\mid y\in\left\{a,b,c\right\}\right\}=\emptyset
. Note that the relation
{\vartriangleleft}
cannot disable events.
Finally, because of the configurations
\left\{a,c\right\},\left\{b,c\right\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\rho_{\gamma}\right)
, neither
a
nor
b
can add a cause to
c
.
Thus we have the state
(\left\{a,b\right\},\to_{\left\{a,b\right\}})
, where
\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},c)\in\to_{\left\{a,b\right\}}\right\}=\emptyset
.
But then, by Definition
6
,
(\left\{a,b\right\},\to_{\left\{a,b\right\}})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{d}}\!(\left\{a,b,c\right\},\to_{\left\{a,b,c\right\}})
for some current causality relations
\to_{\left\{a,b\right\}}
and
\to_{\left\{a,b,c\right\}}
.
Since
\neg\left(\left\{a,b\right\}\mathrel{\mapsto_{1}}\left\{a,b,c\right\}\right)
, this violates our assumption, i.e. there is no DCES that is transition equivalent to
\rho_{\gamma}
.

On the other hand, RCESs cannot distinguish the order of occurrences of events as it is done for the order of
a
and
d
in the DCES in Figure
9
. Hence, both structures are incomparable.

Theorem 34
.

DCESs and RCESs are incomparable.

Proof 6.2
.

The Theorem follows from Lemma
33
and the order insensitivity of the RCESs: There is no RCES with the same behavior as the DCES in Figure
9
since the transition behavior of RCESs is configuration based (Definition
2.3
) and therefore it simply cannot behave differently w.r.t. the order of events in the configuration
\left\{a,d\right\}
.

6.2.
DCESs versus GESs and SESs

By construction, DCESs are at least as expressive as GESs and SESs. To embed a GES (or SES) into a DCES, it suffices to choose
{\vartriangleleft}=\emptyset
(or first
{\blacktriangleright}=\emptyset
and then add those tuples that encode the binary conflict).

{defi}

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES.

The embedding of
\sigma
into a DCES is given by
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\sigma\right)=(E\uplus{\{e_{\text{imp}}\}},{\to}\cup{\{(e_{\text{imp}},e_{\text{imp}})\}},{\vartriangleleft},{\blacktriangleright})
, where
e_{\text{imp}}
is a fresh (and impossible) event and

{{\blacktriangleright}}=\{(a,e_{\text{imp}},b),(b,e_{\text{imp}},a)\mid(a,b)\in\#)\}
.

Similarly, let
\gamma=(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright})
be a GES.

Then, its embedding into a DCES is given by
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\gamma\right)=(E,\to,\emptyset,{\blacktriangleright})
.

In the case of a SES, since
e_{\text{imp}}
is fresh, there are no droppers for the newly added dependencies and thus
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\sigma\right)
is a DCESs in which the order of modifiers does not matter (cf. Definition
D
). Similarly,
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\gamma\right)
is a DCESs in which the order of modifiers does not matter. In the following,
we show that SESs (respectively GESs) and their embeddings are transition equivalent.
Note that, in the proofs, we refer to the technical concept of Single State Dynamic Causality ESs (SSDCs), which are only defined in the Appendix.

Lemma 35
.

Let
\mu
be a GES or SES, then we have
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\mu\right)\simeq_{\operatorname{t}}\mu
.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
\mu
be a GES. We show
(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g^{\prime}}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})\iff(X,\to_{X})\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{d}}\!(Y,\to_{Y})

5
in Definition
6
always holds for
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\mu\right)

are exactly as in Definition

4

\operatorname{i}\!\left(\mu\right)

a\mathrel{\#}b

\mu

\Longleftarrow

X\subset Y

.

. Because

\operatorname{i}\!\left(\mu\right)

(and

) and because of Condition

and

to the respective other event.
Without loss of generality, assume

So,

to the causality relation (by Condition
3
) and, as argued above, this dependency cannot be dropped later.
Therefore,
b
can no longer occur (by Condition
2
). Thus,
Y
is conflict-free.
The condition
\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\left\{e^{\prime}\mid(e^{\prime},e)\in{\to_{X}}\right\}\subseteq X
is a slightly more general version of Condition

. And the last condition holds because of
{\to_{Y}}={\to_{\emptyset}\setminus\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

We conclude that the extension from GESs and SESs to DCESs adds expressive power.

Theorem 36
.

DCESs are strictly more expressive than GESs and SESs.

Proof 6.4
.

With Theorem
34
, DCESs are incomparable to RCESs.
With Theorem
32
and
21
, RCESs are strictly more expressive than GESs and SESs.
With Lemma
35
, DCES is at least as expressive as SESs and GESs.

Now, if DCESs were
not strictly
more expressive than SESs and GESs, then DCESs would become comparable to RCESs, which they are not.

6.3.
DCESs versus EBESs

To show that DCESs are strictly more expressive than EBESs, we use the disabling of GESs and the disjunctive causality of SESs. More precisely, EBESs cannot model the disjunctive causality without a conflict. As counterexample, we use the embedding of the SES
\sigma_{\xi}
in Figure
6
.

Lemma 37
.

There is no EBES with the same configurations as the DCES
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)
, where
\sigma_{\xi}
is the SES given in Figure
6
.

Proof 6.5
.

We consider the embedding
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)
of the SES
\sigma_{\xi}
in Figure
6
, which models disjunctive causality.
According to the Definitions
4
, because
\neg(a\#c)
and
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(a\right)=\operatorname{ic}\!\left(c\right)=\emptyset
, we have
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\{a,c\}
and so
\{a,c\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right)
.
Furthermore, there is no transition
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\{b\}
, because
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(b\right)=\{a\}
.
Yet, there are transitions
\{a\}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\{a,b\}
and
\{c\}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\{c,b\}
, because
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(b\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\{a\},b\right)\subseteq\{a\}
(
\operatorname{ic}\!\left(b\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\{c\},b\right)\subseteq\{c\}
respectively).
The transitions are translated to the embedding according to Lemma
35
and Definition
6
; the same holds for the configurations.

If we assume that there is some EBES
\xi
with the configurations
\emptyset
,
\{a\}
,
\{c\}
,
\{a,c\}
,
\{a,b\}
,
\{b,c\}
and
\{a,b,c\}
, then because of Definition
2.2
and since there is no configuration
\{b\}
, there must be a non-empty bundle
X\!\rightarrowtail\!b
and because of the configurations
\{a,b\},\{b,c\}
, this bundle
X
must contain
a
and
c
. Then, the stability condition of Definition
2.2
implies
a\!\leadsto\!c
and
c\!\leadsto\!a
, so
a
and
c
are in mutual conflict, which contradicts the assumption that we have
\{a,c\}\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\xi\right)
. Thus, there is no EBES with the same configurations as
\operatorname{i}\!\left(\sigma_{\xi}\right).

On the other hand, we formally define an encoding of an EBES into a DCES in Definition
D
in the
Appendix, where disabling uses self-loops of target events, while droppers use auxiliary impossible events (to ensure that they do not intervene with the disabling).
Besides, we construct posets for the configurations of the encoding, and compare them with those of the original EBES.
In this way, we prove that DCESs are at least as expressive as EBESs. But since EBESs cannot model the disjunctive causality—without a conflict—of SESs, DCESs are strictly more expressive than EBESs.

Theorem 38
.

DCESs are strictly more expressive than EBESs.

Proof 6.6
.

Follows from the Lemmata
37
and
53
.

6.4.
HDESs

diagnosis
treatment
dr_
x
_joins
dr_
x
_reads_paper
test

Figure 10.
An HDES example: Dotted arrows denote initially absent dependencies or initially absent dynamic rules.

Higher order Dynamic Causality ESs
(HDESs) are a recent generalization of DCESs
[
13
]
. Figure
10
presents a small example of a second-order addition: After a diagnosis, a treatment becomes possible. Now, a doctor might join the treatment team and nothing changes. However, if the doctor reads—before joining—a medical paper, in which an additional test before the treatment is suggested, the joining of the doctor leads to an additional precondition, namely the test for the treatment. This behavior is modeled with a second-order rule,
\text{dr\_$x$\_reads\_paper}\!\blacktriangleright\![\text{dr\_$x$\_joins}\!\blacktriangleright\![\text{test}\rightarrow\text{treatment}]]
, and the zeroth-order rule
\text{diagnosis}\rightarrow\text{treatment}
.

In general, a HDES consists of a set of events
E
and a set of rules of arbitrary order of dynamics (also arbitrary combination of adding and deletion is allowed).
As a second generalization, it is possible to have sets of events as modifiers (in contrast to the singletons in the DCES approach), this allows for conjunctive adding and dropping where in DCESs only disjunctive modifications were possible. Accordingly, a HDES consists of a set of events and set of dynamic rules (causality is considered as zeroth-order dynamics), whereby this rule set is updated in each transition.

The strict inclusion w.r.t. the expressive power of RCESs into HDESs is proven in
[
13
]
. The proof uses configuration structures that were introduced in
[
11
]
and later relaxed in
[
21
]
. In
[
13
]
, a definition in-between these two is used.

{defi}

[
[
13
]
]
A
configuration structure
is a pair
\mathcal{C}=(E,C)
with
E
a set and
C\subseteq\text{P}\!\left(E\right)
a collection of subsets. We call the elements of
E

events
and the elements of
C

configurations
.
For
x,y
in
C
we write
x\rightarrow_{C}y
if
x\subseteq y
and

\forall Z\,.\;x\subseteq Z\subseteq y\Rightarrow Z\in C.

The relation
\rightarrow_{C}
is called the
step transition relation
.

In
[
13
]
, the authors define a map
\operatorname{hdes}\!\left(\cdot\right)
from any configuration structure (w.r.t. Definition
6.4
) into a HDES and prove that both have the same transition behavior.

Theorem 39

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a configuration structure.
Then the HDES
\Delta=\operatorname{hdes}\!\left(\mathcal{C}\right)
is transition equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
.

Hence, DCESs and RCESs can both be embedded in HDESs. Since DCESs and RCESs are incomparable, then both kinds of structures are strictly less expressive than HDESs.

7.
Conclusions

Iq
ct
cardiac_pacemaker
Iq
rem
Iq
x-ray
(a)
ct
cardiac_pacemaker
rem
Iq
x-ray
(b)
ct
cardiac_pacemaker
rem
Iq
x-ray
(c)

Figure 11.
A DCES, a BES, and a DES modeling the medical example.

We study the idea that causality may change during system runs of event structures. For this, we enhance a simple type of ESs—the rPESs—by means of additional relations capturing the changes in events’ dependencies, driven by the occurrence of other events.

First, in §
4
, we limit our concern to the case where dependencies can only be dropped. We call the new resulting event structure Shrinking Causality ES (SES). We show that the exhibited dynamic causality can be expressed through a completely static perspective, by proving equivalence between SESs and DESs. By such a proof, we do not only show the expressive power of our new ES, but also the big enhancement in expressive power (w.r.t. rPESs) gained by adding only this one relation.

Later on, in §
5
, we study the complementary style where dependencies can be added, resulting in Growing Causality ES (GES). We show that this variant of dynamic causality can model both permanent and temporary disabling. Besides, it can be used to resolve conflicts and, furthermore, to force conflicts. Unlike the SESs, the GESs are not directly comparable to other types of ESs from the literature, except for PESs (and rPESs). GESs cannot model disjunction in the enabling relation but they are able to express conditions for causal relationships.

Finally, in §
6
, we combine both approaches of dynamicity with a new type of event structures, which we call Dynamic Causality ES (DCES).
Therein, dependencies can be both added and dropped. For this new type of ESs, the following two—possibly surprising—facts can be observed:

(1)

There are types of ESs that are incomparable to both SESs and GESs, but that are comparable to (here: strictly less expressive than) DCESs, i.e. the combination of SESs and GESs; one such type is EBESs.

(1)

Though SESs and GESs are strictly less expressive than RCESs, their combination—the newly defined DCESs—is incomparable to RCESs, and incomparable to or even strictly more expressive as any other type of configuration-based ESs.

To highlight the pragmatic advantages of dynamic-causality ESs over other classes of ESs, we refer to the example in the Introduction. Reichert et al. in
[
18
]
emphasize that the model of such processes should distinguish between the regular execution path and the exceptional one. Accordingly, they define two labels,
REGULAR
and
EXCEPTIONAL
, to be assigned to tasks.
Figure
11
(a) shows a DCES model of our example, where
rem
represents the remainder of the treatment process, and
ct
represents the computer tomography.
The initial causality in a DCES e.g.
ct

\rightarrow

rem
corresponds to the regular path of a process, while the changes carried by modifiers e.g.
cardiac_pacemaker
correspond to the exceptional one.
Other static-causality ESs like a BES and a DES can model the fact that either the computer tomography XOR the X-ray is needed, as shown in Figure
11
(b) and
11
(c). The same can be done by an equivalent RCES. However, we argue that none of these models can distinguish between regular and exceptional paths.

Thus, our main contributions are:

(1)

We provide a formal model that allows us to express dynamicity in causality. Using this model, we enhance the rPESs yielding SESs, GESs and DCESs.

(2)

We show the equivalence of SESs and DESs.

(3)

We show the incomparability of GESs to many other types of ESs.

(4)

We show that DCESs are strictly more expressive than EBESs and thus strictly more expressive than many other existing types of ESs.

(5)

We show that DCESs are incomparable to RCESs.

(6)

The new model succinctly supports modern work-flow management systems.

In
[
10
]
, Crafa et al. defined an Event Structure semantics for the
\pi
-calculus based on Prime ESs. Since the latter do not allow for disjunctive causality required for their purpose, and in order to avoid duplications of events, they extended Prime ESs with a set of bound names, and altered the configuration definition to allow for such disjunction. With SESs—that can express disjunctive causality—this problem could possibly be addressed more naturally without copying events. Here, higher-order dynamicity (cf.
[
13
]
) might help to deal with the instantiation of variables caused by communications involving bound names.

Up to now, we limit the execution of DCESs such that an interleaving between adders and droppers of the same causal dependency is forced. As future work, we intend to study the case where modifiers of the same dependency can occur concurrently—read: at the very same instant of time—in DCESs.
Similarly, we want to investigate the situation of concurrent occurrence of an adder and its target in GESs.

More recently,
[
13
]
studies the adding and dropping by sets of events and higher-order dynamics, i.e. events that may change the role of events to adders, droppers or back to normal events.
We are currently developing a web tool (
http://hdes.mtv.tu-berlin.de
) for the modeling and simulation of DCESs and HDESs, containing the example DCESs and HDESs presented in this paper.

The set of possible changes in our newly defined ESs must still be declared statically. In
[
1
]
, the author investigated the idea of
evolutionary
ESs by supporting ad-hoc changes, such that new dependencies as well as events can be added to a structure.

Appendix A
Shrinking Causality

In SESs both notions of configurations, traced-based and transition-based, coincide; and in different situations, the more suitable one can be used.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\sigma
be a SES. Then
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Proof A.1
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
.
By Definition
4
,
C\in\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
implies that there is some
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
such that
\overline{t}\subseteq E
,
\forall 1\leq i,j\leq n\,.\;\neg\left(e_{i}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}\right)
,
\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
, and
C=\overline{t}
.
Hence, by Definition
4
,
\overline{t_{i}}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\overline{t_{i+1}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\left\{e_{1}\right\}
.
Thus, by Definition
4
,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

By Definition
4
,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
implies that there are
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subseteq E
such that
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!X_{1}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!\ldots\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!X_{n}
and
X_{n}=C
.
Then, by Definition
4
, we have:

(C1)

\displaystyle\emptyset\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq X_{n}\subseteq E

(C2)

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\in X_{n}\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)

(C3)

\displaystyle\forall e\in X_{1}\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\emptyset,e\right)\right)\subseteq\emptyset

(C4)

\displaystyle\forall 1\leq i<n\,.\;\forall e\in X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X_{i},e\right)\right)\subseteq X_{i}

Let
X_{1}=\left\{e_{1,1},\ldots,e_{1,m_{1}}\right\}
and
X_{i}\setminus X_{i-1}=\left\{e_{i,1},\ldots,e_{i,m_{i}}\right\}
for all
1<i\leq n
.
Then by Definition
4
,
t=e_{1,1}\cdots e_{1,m_{1}}\cdots e_{n,1}\cdots e_{n,m_{n}}=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdots e_{k}^{\prime}
is a trace such that
\overline{t}\subseteq E
(because of (
C1
)),
\neg\left(e_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\#}e_{j}^{\prime}\right)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
(because of (
C1
) and (
C2
)), for all
1\leq i\leq k
and all
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
we have
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i,j}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i,j}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
(because of (
C3
) and (
C4
)), and
\overline{t}=C
(because
X_{n}=C
).
Thus
C\in\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Moreover the following technical Lemma relates transitions and the extension of traces by causally independent events.

Lemma 41
.

Let
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
be a SES and
X,Y\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Then
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
iff there are
t_{1}=e_{1}\cdots e_{n},t_{2}=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}e_{n+1}\cdots e_{n+m}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
such that
X=\overline{t_{1}}
,
Y=\overline{t_{2}}
, and
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)\subseteq X
.

Proof A.2
.

By Definition
4
and Lemma
40
,
X\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
implies that there is a trace
t_{1}=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
such that
X=\overline{t_{1}}
.

If
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
, then by Definition
4
, we have
X\subseteq Y
,
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\,.\;\neg(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime})
, and
\forall e\in Y\setminus X\,.\;(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right))\subseteq X
. Then by Definition
4
,
t_{2}=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}e_{n+1}\cdots e_{n+m}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and
Y=\overline{t_{2}}
for an arbitrary linearization
e_{n+1}\cdots e_{n+m}
of the events in
Y\setminus X
, i.e. with
\left\{e_{n+1},\ldots,e_{n+m}\right\}=Y\setminus X
such that
e_{n+i}\neq e_{n+j}
whenever
1\leq i,j,\leq m
and
i\neq j
.

If there is a trace
t_{2}=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}e_{n+1}\cdots e_{n+m}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
such that
Y=\overline{t_{2}}
and
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)\subseteq X
then
X\subseteq Y
. Moreover, by Definition
4
,
t_{2}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
implies
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\,.\;\neg\left(e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}\right)
. Thus by Definition
4
,
X\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{s}}\!Y
.

Note that the condition
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in Y\setminus X\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(X,e\right)\right)\subseteq X
states that the events in
Y\setminus X
are causally independent from each other.

In Definition
4.2
we provide an encoding of a DESs into a SESs.
Of course it can be criticized that the encoding adds events (although they are fresh and impossible). But as the following example—with more bundles than events—shows it is not always possible to translate a DES into a SES without additional impossible events.

Lemma 42
.

There are DESs
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
, as e.g.
\delta=\left(\left\{a,b,c,d,e\right\},\emptyset,\rightarrowtail\right)
with
\rightarrowtail\;=\left\{\left\{x,y\right\}\!\rightarrowtail\!e\mid x,y\in\left\{a,b,c,d\right\}\land x\neq y\right\}
, that cannot be translated into a SES
\sigma=\left(E,\#^{\prime},\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.

Proof A.3
.

Assume a SES
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
such that
E=\{a,b,c,d,

e\}
and
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
.
According to §
2.2
,
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
contains all
sequences of distinct events of
E
such that
e
is not the first, second, or third event, i.e. for
e
to occur in a trace it has to be preceded by at least three of the other events.
Since by Definition
4
conflicts cannot be dropped,
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
implies
\#^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, since
e
has to be preceded by at least three other events that can occur in any order,
\to
has to contain at least three initial causes for
e
. Without loss of generality let
a\!\to\!e
,
b\!\to\!e
, and
c\!\to\!e
.
Because of the traces
abd,acd\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
, we need the droppers
d\!\vartriangleleft\![b\rightarrow e]
and
d\!\vartriangleleft\![c\rightarrow e]
. Then
ad\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
but
ad\notin\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
.
In fact if we fix
E=\left\{a,b,c,d,e\right\}
there are only finitely many different SESs
\sigma=\left(E,\#^{\prime},\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
and for none of them
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
holds.

Note that the above lemma implies that no encoding of the above DES can result into a SES with the same events such that the DES and its encoding have same configurations or posets.

Appendix B
Alternative Partial Order Semantics in DES and SES

To show that DES and SES are not only behavioral equivalent ES models but are also very closely related at the structural level we consider the remaining four intensional partial order semantics for DES of
[
17
]
.

Liberal causality is the least restrictive notion of causality in
[
17
]
. Here each set of events from bundles pointing to an event
e
that satisfies all bundles pointing to
e
is a cause.

{defi}

[Liberal Causality]
Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES,
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
one of its traces,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.
A set
U
is a cause of
e_{i}
in
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists 1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
,

•

U\subseteq\left(X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{m}\right)
, and

•

\forall 1\leq k\leq m\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{lib}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for a trace
t
.

Bundle satisfaction causality is based on the idea that for an event
e
in a trace each bundle pointing to
e
is satisfied by exactly one event in a cause of
e
.

{defi}

[Bundle Satisfaction Causality]
Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES,
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
one of its traces,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.
A set
U
is a cause of
e_{i}
in
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists 1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
and

•

there is a surjective mapping
f:\left\{X_{k}\right\}\to U
such that
f\!\left(X_{k}\right)\in X_{k}
for all
1\leq k\leq m
.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{bsat}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for a trace
t
.

Minimal causality requires that there is no subset which is also a cause.

{defi}

[Minimal Causality]
Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES and let
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
be one of its traces,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.
A set
U
is a cause of
e_{i}
in
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists 1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
,

•

\forall 1\leq k\leq m\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
, and

•

there is no proper subset of
U
satisfying the previous two conditions.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{min}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for a trace
t
.

Late causality contains the latest causes of an event that form a minimal set.

{defi}

[Late Causality]
Let
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
be a DES,
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
one of its traces,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.
A set
U
is a cause of
e_{i}
in
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
if

•

\forall e\in U\,.\;\exists 1\leq j<i\,.\;e=e_{j}
,

•

\forall 1\leq k\leq m\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
,

•

there is no proper subset of
U
satisfying the previous two conditions, and

•

U
is the latest set satisfying the previous three conditions.

Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{late}}\!\left(t\right)
be the set of posets obtained this way for a trace
t
.

As derived in
[
17
]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{late}}\!\left(t\right),\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{d}}\!\left(t\right)\subseteq\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{min}}\!\left(t\right)\subseteq\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{bsat}}\!\left(t\right)\subseteq\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{lib}}\!\left(t\right)

for all traces
t
.
Moreover a behavioral partial order semantics is defined and it is shown that two DESs have the same posets w.r.t. the behavioral partial order semantics iff they have the same posets w.r.t. the early partial order semantics iff they have the same traces.

Bundle satisfaction causality is—as the name suggests—closely related to the existence of bundles. In SESs there are no bundles. Of course, as shown by the encoding
\operatorname{des}\!\left(\cdot\right)
in Definition
4.2
, we can transform the initial and dropped causes of an event into a bundle. And of course if we do so an SES
\sigma
and its encoding
\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)
have exactly the same families of posets. But because bundles are no native concept of SESs, we cannot directly map the definition of posets w.r.t. bundle satisfaction to SESs.

To adapt the definitions of posets in the other three cases we have to replace the condition
U\subseteq\left(X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{m}\right)
by
U\subseteq\left(\left\{e\mid e\!\to\!e_{i}\lor\exists e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]\right\}\right)
and replace the condition
\forall 1\leq k\leq m\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
by
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq U
(as in Definition
4
). The remaining conditions remain the same with respect to traces as defined in Definition
4
.
Let
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{lib}}\!\left(t\right)
,
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{min}}\!\left(t\right)
, and
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{late}}\!\left(t\right)
denote the sets of posets obtained this way for a trace
t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
of a SES
\sigma
w.r.t. liberal, minimal, and late causality. Moreover, let
\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(\delta\right)=\bigcup_{t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)}{\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(t\right)}
and
\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\bigcup_{t\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)}{\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(t\right)}
for all
x\in\left\{\operatorname{lib},\operatorname{bsat},\operatorname{min},\operatorname{late}\right\}
.

Since again the definitions of posets in DESs and SESs are very similar the encodings
\operatorname{des}\!\left(\cdot\right)
and
\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\cdot\right)
preserve families of posets. The proof is very similar to the proofs of the Theorems
11
and
13
.

Theorem 43
.

For each SES
\sigma
there is a DES
\delta
, namely
\delta=\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)
, and for each DES
\delta
there is a SES
\sigma
, namely
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)
, such that
\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{P}_{x}\!\left(\delta\right)
for all
x\in\left\{\operatorname{lib},\operatorname{min},\operatorname{late}\right\}
.

Proof B.1
.

The definitions of posets in DESs and SESs w.r.t. minimal and late causality differ in exactly the same condition and its replacement as the definitions of posets in DESs and SESs w.r.t. early causality. Thus the proof in these two cases is similar to the proofs of the Theorems
11
and
13
.

If
\sigma=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
is a SES then, by Lemmas
9
and
10
,
\delta=\operatorname{des}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
is a DES such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)
and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)
.
If
\delta=\left(E,\#,\rightarrowtail\right)
is a DES then, by Lemma
12
,
\sigma=\operatorname{ses}\!\left(\delta\right)=\left(E,\#,\to,{\vartriangleleft}\right)
is a DES such that
\operatorname{T}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
and
\operatorname{C}\!\left(\delta\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\sigma\right)
.
In both cases let
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}\in\operatorname{T}\!\left(\sigma\right)
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
X_{1}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i},\ldots,X_{m}\!\rightarrowtail\!e_{i}
be all bundles pointing to
e_{i}
.

In the case of liberal causality, for
U
to be a cause for
e_{i}
the definition of posets in SESs requires
U\subseteq\left(\left\{e\mid e\!\to\!e_{i}\lor\exists e^{\prime}\in E\,.\;e\!\vartriangleleft\![e^{\prime}\rightarrow e_{i}]\right\}\right)
and
(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\setminus\operatorname{dc}\!\left(U,e_{i}\right))\subseteq U
.
The second condition holds iff
\forall 1\leq k\leq m\,.\;X_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
as shown in the proofs of the Theorems
11
and
13
.
By the Definitions
4.2
and
4.2
, the first conditions holds iff
U\subseteq\left(X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{m}\right)
.
So, by the definitions of posets in DESs and SESs w.r.t. liberal causality,
\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{lib}}\!\left(\sigma\right)=\operatorname{P}_{\operatorname{lib}}\!\left(\delta\right)
.

Appendix C
Growing Causality

As in SESs, both notions of configurations of GESs, traced-based and
transition-based coincide; in different situations the more suitable one can be used.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\gamma
be a GES. Then
\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
.

Proof C.1
.

Let
\gamma=\left(E,\to,{\blacktriangleright}\right)
.

By Definition
5
,
C\in\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)
implies that there is some
t=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
such that
\overline{t}\subseteq E
,
\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\forall 1\leq i\leq n\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i}\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
, and
C=\overline{t}
. Hence by Definition
5
,
\overline{t_{i}}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!\overline{t_{i+1}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!\left\{e_{1}\right\}
. Thus by Definition
5
,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
.

By Definition
5
,
C\in\operatorname{C}\!\left(\gamma\right)
implies that there are
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subseteq E
such that
\emptyset\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!X_{1}\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!\ldots\!\mapsto_{\operatorname{g}}\!X_{n}
and
X_{n}=C
.
Then by Definition
5
, we have:

(D1)

\displaystyle\emptyset\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq X_{n}\subseteq E

(D2)

\displaystyle\forall e\in X_{1}\,.\;\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\emptyset,e\right)\right)\subseteq\emptyset

(D3)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\forall 1\leq i<n\,.\;\forall e\in X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}\,.\\
\hskip 30.00005pt\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(X_{i},e\right)\right)\subseteq X_{i}\end{array}

(D4)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\forall 1\leq i<n\,.\;\forall t,m\in X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}\,.\;\forall c\in E\,.\\
\hskip 30.00005ptm\!\blacktriangleright\![c\rightarrow t]\implies c\in X_{i}\end{array}

Let
X_{1}=\left\{e_{1,1},\ldots,e_{1,m_{1}}\right\}
and
X_{i}\setminus X_{i-1}=\left\{e_{i,1},\ldots,e_{i,m_{i}}\right\}
for all
1<i\leq n
.
Then by Definition
5
,
t=e_{1,1}\cdots e_{1,m_{1}}\cdots e_{n,1}\cdots e_{n,m_{n}}=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdots e_{k}^{\prime}
is a trace such that
\overline{t}\subseteq E
(because of (
D1
)), for all
1\leq i\leq k
and all
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
we have
\left(\operatorname{ic}\!\left(e_{i,j}\right)\cup\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i,j}\right)\right)\subseteq\overline{t_{i-1}}
(because of (
D2
), (
D3
), and, by (
D4
),
\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}}\cup X_{i},e_{i,j}\right)=\operatorname{ac}\!\left(\overline{t_{i-1}},e_{i,j}\right)
), and
\overline{t}=C
(because
X_{n}=C
).
Thus
C\in\operatorname{C}_{\text{Tr}}\!\left(\gamma\right)
.

Appendix D
Dynamic Causality

To compare DCESs to other ESs we define the Single State Dynamic Causality ESs (SSDCs) as a subclass of DCESs in that no modifier can add and drop the same dependency.

{defi}

Let SSDC be a subclass of DCESs such that
\varrho
is a SSDC iff

\hbox to7.79pt{\vbox to8.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.59207pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
